- 2026-07-31 · bug · (#33) the accepted residual from #15/#31 — `archivePage`/`overwriteOutputFile` resolved a real, validated destination and then handed the PATH STRING to `writeFileSync`/`renameSync`, so a directory component swapped between the resolution and the write redirected un-redacted capture (or dossier) bytes outside the validated real root — was reproduced with a `node:fs` spy that plants the swap deterministically inside whichever low-level call actually performs the write (no wall-clock race), then closed with fd-anchored writes: `openNewOutputFileSafely`/`writeNewOutputFile` (`src/fs/output-root.ts`) open the real path `wx` and only trust it once TWO independent post-open checks both hold — no directory component is a symlink, and the open fd's `{dev, ino}` still matches what the path names. Neither check alone is sound: the component walk alone is exactly today's check-then-use, and the inode check alone is fooled by a symlink an attacker leaves in place, since `lstatSync` follows the same symlinked directory component `open` already did. `overwriteOutputFile`'s temp-then-rename gained the analogous directory-realpath re-verification immediately before the rename. CLOSED: un-redacted content can no longer be written outside the validated real root via a component swap — the bytes go through a proven fd. REMAINING, honestly stated rather than claimed closed: (1) an actor who wins the PRE-OPEN window can still cause an EMPTY file to be created at a location of their choosing before the run fails closed and writes no content there — pure Node has no `openat`/`O_NOFOLLOW`; (2) `overwriteOutputFile`'s `renameSync` stays a path operation with no `renameat`, so the reports leaf keeps a narrower rename-time window, now narrowed by the new check rather than erased
- 2026-07-31 · note · (#33, pre-existing, out of scope) `archivePage` still writes the html leaf before the provenance leaf, so a race refusal on the SECOND write (provenance) leaves the first (html) sitting on disk — inside the validated root, unaffected by this fix and unchanged from before it
- 2026-07-31 · bug · (#33, PR #48, Codex adversarial review round 1, rated critical) the fd-anchoring proof was bypassable with a HARD LINK: the two post-open checks establish "the fd is what this path names", which is NOT the same claim as "this path is the fd's only name". An actor who wins the pre-open window (file created through a symlinked component, outside the root) can restore the real parent and hard-link that outside file back to the in-root path — every check then passes HONESTLY, because it is one inode under two names, one of them outside the root. Closed with a `nlink !== 1` refusal, which is exact rather than heuristic: `openSync(..., "wx")` is `O_CREAT|O_EXCL`, so it only ever succeeds by CREATING the file, and a newly created regular file has exactly one link — any second name appeared after our open and is by definition not ours, so the check refuses no legitimate configuration. The general shape is the one this repo keeps re-learning one level up from where it last learned it: #31 established that a LEXICAL check does not answer where the bytes land, and this round establishes that an INODE check does not answer how many names those bytes are reachable under
- 2026-07-31 · bug · (#33, PR #48, Codex adversarial review round 1, rated critical) the verification-failure cleanup was a bare `unlinkSync(realPath)`, and `unlink` resolves directory components like every other path call — so when verification failed BECAUSE a parent was swapped for a symlink, the cleanup followed that same symlink and deleted whatever sat at the corresponding name in the attacker-chosen directory. A guard that refuses to WRITE outside the root while happily DELETING outside it is not a smaller version of the same control, it is a different and worse bug — the fix that introduced it was written to avoid leaving stray files behind, i.e. tidiness bought data loss. Now inode-guarded: cleanup lstats the path, compares `{dev, ino}` against the fd's, and unlinks only its own inode, leaving anything else strictly alone. Cost of failing safe here is a stray EMPTY file in a directory the attacker already controls

The test for it is worth noting as a shape, and the shape was not what it was first written up as: removing the guard does NOT hang the suite and does NOT fail an assertion — the vitest worker dies of an OUT-OF-MEMORY crash (`ERR_IPC_CHANNEL_CLOSED`, v8 `OOMErrorHandler`), because the `vi.fn()` wrapper records the arguments of every call and the infinite loop exhausts the heap recording them. So the mutation check for an infinite-loop guard reads as a worker crash, which is easy to misdiagnose as flaky infrastructure and dismiss. Recorded because the first draft of this very line asserted "it hangs" from reasoning, and running it said otherwise

- 2026-07-31 · bug · (#17, PR B, Codex adversarial review round 1, rated high) the pair-first placement loop had the SAME defect twice, in two different disguises, and only the reviewer caught the second. Round 0 (my own): walking slot order once let a weak pair claim a player before a stronger partnership could. My fix — propose one winner per slot, commit the strongest, re-propose — fixed that and left the deeper version intact: each pair contended only for its MODAL slot, and losers cascaded AFTERWARDS into whatever was left, contesting nobody. So a cascader was silently exempt from the collision rule the heuristic is built on: a pair that lost D1 could not contend for D2 against D2's claimant however much better its combined rating. The real fix is to re-derive each pair's CURRENT preference (best still-open shared slot) every round, which makes a cascade a genuine re-entry into contention rather than a consolation placement. The shape to remember: my fix addressed the symptom I had a failing test for (a split partnership) rather than the mechanism underneath it (contention computed once, from a static preference) — the fifth-plus occurrence in this repo of fixing the named instance rather than the class, and the first where the instance and the class were separated by only one round of my own work

The generalization that finally holds: an output boundary is a (surface × outcome) pair, not a surface. Error paths are systematically the ones missed because they are written as an afterthought to the happy path, they are where UNTRUSTED INPUT IS MOST LIKELY TO BE QUOTED BACK verbatim (that is what makes a diagnostic useful), and they are the least-tested. - 2026-07-31 · bug · (#17, PR B, Codex adversarial review round 11, rated medium) `addEvent` validated its range only against the input, never against what already depended on it: moving or narrowing an existing event's dates left availability rows attached to an event that no longer contained their day. `setAvailability` enforces day-within-range at WRITE time, so nothing would ever notice — the corruption is invisible until someone reads per-event-day availability and finds days the event never covered. The general shape: an idempotent "update in place" writer inherits responsibility for every invariant its earlier writes established, and a validation that runs on the way IN does not cover a later edit to the thing being validated against. Refusing was chosen over deleting the rows or silently keeping them because which of those the operator meant is genuinely ambiguous, and the diagnostic names the days so they can decide — the same "error with candidates, never guess" posture GRAMMAR.md takes for an ambiguous target

First, the quality gate has no check for this at all: tests pass, `parity_check.rb` compares command names and summaries, and neither reads prose — so a PR that changes behavior can leave every doc in the repo lying and still be green. Second, the cost is not cosmetic: the specific failures were a runbook telling operators the delivered feature did not exist, a runbook telling them to verify a write through a surface that never showed values, and a comment justifying correct behavior with a false reason. Candidate for the ace findings pipeline: a "docs mention this symbol" check, or simply a standing `invoke`/`verify` step that greps the docs for every identifier and phrase the diff touched
- 2026-07-31 · bug · (#17, PR B, Codex adversarial review round 12, rated high) the range guard I added in round 11 was itself a check-then-act: it read the dependent availability rows, then upserted the event as a separate autocommit statement. `better-sqlite3` is synchronous so nothing interleaves within one event loop — and that is exactly the reasoning that makes this class invisible, because the threat is not one loop, it is TWO PROCESSES. nadal runs `tn mcp serve` against the same WAL database a CLI invocation uses, so an agent chat writing availability and a terminal correcting event dates are genuinely concurrent, and a row committed between the check and the update is stranded by the very update the guard polices. Fixed with `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` (not the default deferred, which takes the write lock only on the first write and so loses precisely the race it is there to win). Two things worth carrying: a fix for a race can reintroduce the race one level up — this is the SECOND check-then-act in the same 60-line function, after round 4's `created` flag — and "the runtime is single-threaded" is an argument about a process, never about a deployment
- 2026-07-31 · process · (#17, PR B) the two-connection test for the above was itself a near-false-green, caught only by mutation-checking it. `better-sqlite3`'s `verbose` hook fires JUST BEFORE each statement executes, so injecting the competing write when `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` is announced happens while the lock is still unheld — the injected write succeeds and the test reports a correct fix as broken. Injecting on the NEXT statement is what actually observes the held lock. The general point is about concurrency tests specifically: they are written against a mental model of when a lock is acquired, that model is usually off by one step, and the failure is silent in both directions (a green that proves nothing, or a red against working code). Mutating the fix — flipping `immediate` back to `deferred` and confirming the test goes red — is the only thing that distinguishes them, and it took thirty seconds
- 2026-07-31 · bug · (#17, PR B, Codex adversarial review round 13, rated high) round 12 serialized `addEvent` and I treated the invariant as closed; the reviewer pointed out the race runs BOTH DIRECTIONS. `setAvailability` also read-then-wrote: resolve an event and its range, let another process narrow that event — which finds nothing to strand, because nothing is committed yet — and the upsert lands a row outside the range that now exists. Same corrupt state, opposite order, and the guard on the other side cannot see it. A mutual invariant between two writers needs serializing at BOTH writers; locking one and reasoning "the other now cannot interleave" is exactly backwards, because the unlocked one is free to start first. Both are now `BEGIN IMMEDIATE`, so whichever commits first the other observes committed state
- 2026-07-31 · process · (#17, PR B) my mutation check on the round-13 test reported "mutant survived" and it was a lie: the command was `git add -A && git commit -q -m ... && python3 <mutate>`, the commit failed (a 1Password signing hiccup), and `&&` meant the MUTATION NEVER RAN — so the suite passed against unmutated code and looked exactly like a false green. I nearly recorded a good test as worthless. Two rules: a mutation check must VERIFY the mutation is in place before running the suite (print the changed line, or assert on the file contents — one line of output), and chaining a mutation behind an unrelated command with `&&` puts the whole check at the mercy of a step that has nothing to do with it. The failure mode is the worst kind: it reports the safe-looking answer ("your test is weak") rather than an error
- 2026-07-31 · bug · (#17, PR B, Codex adversarial review round 14, rated medium) my conflict resolver left a literal `||||||| <sha>` marker in the findings log, because it was written for the three-marker conflict style and this repo is configured `merge.conflictStyle = zdiff3`, which emits FOUR: `<<<<<<<`, `|||||||`, `=======`, `>>>>>>>`. The script took everything between `<<<<<<<` and `=======` as "our side", which in zdiff3 is ours PLUS the marker PLUS the common ancestor — so it did not merely miss a marker, it copied the ancestor section into the file. My verification then confirmed the wrong thing: I grepped for the three markers I knew about and got zero. Two rules. Resolve conflicts with a tool that reads the repo's configured style rather than a hand-rolled parser (`git checkout --ours/--theirs`, `git merge-file`, or just an editor), and — the cheap universal backstop — run `git diff --check` after ANY resolution: it knows every marker style, it is one command, and it caught this in the reviewer's first pass after I had already declared the merge verified with a full green suite, a fresh migrate and an end-to-end report build. A green test suite says nothing about a corrupted markdown file
- 2026-07-31 · fact · (#33, PR #48 delivery) commit signing on this machine is doing less than it looks like it is doing, and it blocked an unattended run to do it: 1Password's `op-ssh-sign` locked mid-`ship`, failing every commit with `1Password: failed to fill whole buffer` while the run held finished, green, twice-reviewed work. Checking what the signatures actually bought, rather than assuming: `main` has NO branch protection and NO rulesets, so nothing requires them; the GitHub API reports every feature-branch commit as `verified: false, reason: unknown_key` because the SSH key is not registered as a SIGNING key on the account; `git log --show-signature` cannot verify locally either (`~/.ssh/allowed_signers` does not exist); and squash-merge discards those commits anyway, replacing them with a merge commit GitHub signs itself (`verified: true, reason: valid`). So the control was costing availability and returning provenance nobody could check — the shape worth remembering is that a security control which FAILS LOUDLY feels like it is working, and "is anything actually verifying this?" is a one-API-call question that went unasked until it broke something. Fallback policy recorded in `PROJECT.md` → *Branch & PR Policy*, deliberately as a per-commit visible retry rather than a config flip, and with its three expiry conditions named

Neither assertion was wrong; both NAMES were. So the cheap check, on every test title and comment: underline the nouns, and if one of them is a module the assertion does not touch, the claim is wider than the test — narrow the claim or widen the test, and prefer narrowing. - 2026-07-31 · decision · (#18, PR #54 round 2, Codex adversarial review High finding B, ruled by the HC) `addMatchFromScorecard`'s parent-match matching is deliberately MORE LENIENT than `upsertTeamMatch`'s: `scheduledTime`/`site` fill in blanks instead of requiring exact (normalized) equality — a blank on EITHER side (stored or the incoming payload) is compatible with anything on the other, and `stored ?? incoming` is what gets written, so a later re-ingest can fill in a detail the first capture omitted, or omit a detail the first capture already supplied, without nulling it out. Scoped to `match-add` only (`upsertTeamMatch` and the `team-pull`/TennisRecord scraping path are untouched) and reuses `normalizeTimeKey`/`normalizeSiteKey` rather than a second hand-rolled equality. **Accepted risk, ruled by the HC, not hidden:** a genuine SECOND same-day fixture between the same two teams whose FIRST ingest omitted a discriminator will now be silently absorbed into the first match on a later ingest rather than creating the second a strict comparison would have — possible ONLY when the stored side is blank (two ingests that both supply a value still discriminate correctly; the doubleheader guard PR #31 added survives intact and is tested). Traded for in-event ergonomics: a scorekeeper correcting a scorecard mid-event should not also have to know whether every discriminator was captured perfectly the first time. See `src/ingest/match-add.ts`'s `upsertLenientTeamMatchForScorecard` doc comment for the full statement
- 2026-07-31 · process · (#18, PR #54 round 3, Codex adversarial review High finding 1) the risk-boundary claim in the entry directly above is FALSE, and the shape is the exact one `docs/findings.md:195` already named — a true-sounding sentence read one inference too far. "Possible ONLY when the STORED side is blank" is wrong: `compatible` accepts a blank on EITHER side, so the absorption risk runs in BOTH directions — a later fixture that OMITS a discriminator can be absorbed into an EARLIER one that SUPPLIED it, exactly as readily as an earlier blank is filled in by a later supplied value. `compatible` has no notion of "first" or "second" ingest, only of which side (if either) is blank right now. The only combination that reliably discriminates two genuinely different fixtures is BOTH sides SUPPLIED and DIFFERENT after normalization — every other combination touching a blank is, by the function's own design, treated as the same match. This entry does not retract the underlying HC decision (the trade itself stands, and PR #31's doubleheader guard is still intact and tested) — only the earlier mischaracterization of how far it reaches. Corrected wording lives in `src/ingest/match-add.ts`'s `upsertLenientTeamMatchForScorecard` doc comment; this file stays append-only, so the entry above is left as originally written rather than edited in place

- 2026-07-31 · fact · (#49 verify) `src/ingest/upsert.ts` is classified BINARY by every text tool on this machine (`file` reports `data`), because `NULL_KEY = "\0null"` embeds a literal 0x00 byte in the source rather than the two-character escape. The file is valid UTF-8 and the sentinel is deliberate and correct — the cost is tooling: any grep run with `-I` (skip binary files) silently reports ZERO matches in it, and the Claude Code shell's `grep` wrapper passes `-I` by default. During `verify` I ran a `grep -rn "teamMemberships" src/` to audit every read site of a table I had just changed, and `upsert.ts` — the file containing the new write primitive — was invisible in the results. A missing file in a grep result looks exactly like a file that legitimately does not match, so the failure mode is a silently INCOMPLETE audit that reads as a complete one. Two rules: when a grep-based audit is load-bearing, cross-check the file LIST (`git ls-files` / a language-aware search) against the hit list rather than trusting hit counts, and prefer `\0` (escape) over a raw NUL byte in source when a sentinel needs one, so the file stays text to the whole toolchain. Not changed here — it is pre-existing (present since the `NULL_KEY` commit, unrelated to #49) and changing a sentinel's bytes deserves its own change with its own test

The shape underneath all of them: a set that COULD hold more than one row, selected from via `.find()` / `[0]` / equivalent with no check that it held exactly one. Fixing the reported instance never closed the class, because the report names a symptom (this court, this slot, this candidate set) and the class lives one level up, in the shape of the selection itself. What finally closed it was not a smarter fix but a different KIND of check: grep every `.find(`, `[0]`, and bare-array-index selection against a set built from a query, then check — for each one — the actual `.unique()` / `uniqueIndex` declaration on the relevant column in `src/db/schema.ts`, not a prose comment or a recollection of "this can't happen." A DB-level uniqueness constraint is the only thing that proves a `.find()`/`[0]` pick safe; everything else is an assumption standing in for a guarantee that was never actually checked

- 2026-07-31 · process · (#46, PR #52) the branch-protection PreToolUse hook (`.claude/hooks/enforce-branch-creation.sh`) DEADLOCKS an agent that starts a rebase: mid-rebase HEAD is detached, the hook reads a detached HEAD as a protected branch and fails closed, and it matches on the string `git rebase` — so it refuses `--continue` AND `--abort` AND `--quit`, which are the only commands that can leave the state it is objecting to. The work was never at risk (the branch ref stays at its pre-rebase value until a rebase completes, so all four commits and the pushed remote were intact throughout), but the run could not self-recover and needed the HC to run `git rebase --abort` outside the tool. The hook's own header states the intent it violates here — "on every other branch an agent is free to write, commit, and push at any time; this guard must never get in the way there" — and this run WAS on a feature branch the entire time. The workaround that kept the run moving was integrating with `git merge` instead, which never detaches HEAD; that is a real alternative rather than a bypass, and it is what surfaced the merge-born defect above. The general shape: a fail-closed guard whose predicate is a TRANSIENT state must not also block the transition OUT of that state, or it converts a safe refusal into a trap. Left unfixed here on purpose — the guard is shared config governing every agent in the repo, so changing it is not in scope for a bug fix about team identity, and it wants its own issue rather than a fold

Raised against #46's migration-recovery message, but the reachability check says it is not that message's defect: the identical corruption is demonstrable on the SUCCESS path, `db migrate status=ok path="..."`, which contains no code from this PR — a `TN_DB_PATH` holding a literal newline prints as `path=".../we ird.db"`, naming a file that does not exist. It is a property of the one-line `key=value` summary contract itself and applies to every path-bearing field of every command, so fixing it means either validating configured paths at the boundary or choosing a lossless encoding for the summary — a CLI-wide decision that does not belong inside a bug fix about team identity. What WAS accepted from the same finding is narrower and real: the new no-control-characters assertion hand-listed a character range while claiming to enforce "single-line", missing DEL, the whole C1 block and every `\p{Cf}` format control — a check whose comment outran what it enforced, so it now uses `sanitizeValue`'s OWN class (`\p{Cc}\p{Cf}` plus U+2028/U+2029). The shape worth keeping: when a guard's job is "matches what module X strips", the predicate must BE module X's predicate, not a hand-copied subset that drifts the moment X widens
- 2026-07-31 · bug · (#46, PR #52, Codex adversarial review round 4, rated high) the recovery runbook told the reader to restore captain notes by pasting exported CSV values into `tn player note '<player>' "<note>"`. A captain note is arbitrary text a human typed, so it can carry apostrophes, quotes, backslashes, newlines, or `$(...)`/backticks — and inside DOUBLE quotes a shell evaluates the last of those. The documented recovery could therefore mangle, drop, or EXECUTE the very content it exists to preserve, and an apostrophe in an ordinary name (`O'Brien`) breaks the single-quoted forms too. Checked rather than assumed on both sides: `sqlite3 -csv` quotes and doubles correctly, so the EXPORT is lossless (a note with embedded quotes, commas and newlines round-trips through a real CSV parser intact) — the hazard is entirely on the way back IN. Fixed by directing anything non-trivial through the `player_note` MCP tool, which takes the note as a structured JSON argument so no shell ever parses it, and which is independently the only route that can restore a PAIRING note; the CLI forms stay documented but are explicitly scoped to simple values and switched from double to single quotes. The general shape, and the reason this was a doc bug rather than a code bug: a runbook that says "substitute your data here" IS an interface, and the data it substitutes is as untrusted as any other input — round 4 found it in prose in the same PR whose code had already been hardened three times

That sequence is the repo's own most-repeated shape arriving in the test harness itself: patching the file that FAILED left the class open, and the class re-presented immediately through two different files. The cause is not slow code — it is honest work colliding with parallel load (real on-disk SQLite, real `tn` subprocesses, whole-HTML tokenization), all of it comfortably under 5s alone and over it once eight workers compete. Fixed as ONE number in ONE place, `testTimeout: 30_000` in vitest.config.ts, and the per-file override added earlier in this same PR was REMOVED rather than left beside it: a per-test timeout equal to the global is dead config that reads as a special case to every later reader. 30s is ~6.6x the slowest observed case, so a genuine hang still fails fast. Verified by three consecutive full green runs rather than one
- 2026-07-31 · bug · (#46, PR #52, Codex adversarial review round 6, rated high) the fail-safe added in round 5 rendered an unrenderable database path with `JSON.stringify` and CLAIMED that was lossless. It is not: `JSON.stringify` escapes the C0 controls but leaves DEL, the whole C1 block, every `\p{Cf}` format control and U+2028/U+2029 LITERAL — and `sanitizeValue` replaces precisely those with spaces one layer downstream. So a path containing U+2028 or RIGHT-TO-LEFT OVERRIDE reached the reader neither losslessly nor control-free while the message asserted both, i.e. a claim outrunning what the code enforced — the same shape logged at :177-178 and :183, now in its third distinct costume in this one PR. The galling part: `src/cli/emit.ts` ALREADY documents this exact `JSON.stringify` shortfall (it was written after a bidi override reached a terminal through `team show --json`), so the knowledge was in the repo, one directory away, and was not applied. Closed with `losslessPath`, which escapes every character in `sanitizeValue`'s OWN class to `\u{XXXX}` and escapes backslashes FIRST so a path legitimately containing the text `\u{2028}` cannot be confused with an escape the renderer produced (the same ordering argument `quoteSummaryValue` already makes). Regressions cover one Zl-adjacent character (U+2028) and one Cf (U+202E), asserting `sanitizeValue(message) === message` rather than a hand-listed absence — the fixed-point assertion is the only form that cannot drift from the sanitizer. The transferable rule: 'escape it' is not a property, it is a property RELATIVE TO A CONSUMER — the correct escaper is the one whose character class is the consumer's, and any other choice is a guess that happens to look safe
- 2026-07-31 · bug · (#46, PR #52, Codex adversarial review round 7, rated high) `losslessPath` and the fail-safe branch were both derived from `sanitizeValue`'s character class — deliberately, as round 4's own lesson ("a guard whose job is 'matches what module X strips' must call X"). That instinct was right and the conclusion was still wrong, which is the interesting part: `sanitizeValue` is only ONE of two mechanisms between the message and the reader. The other is Node's UTF-8 encoder, which destroys a LONE UTF-16 SURROGATE — `\p{Cs}`, a class the sanitizer does not touch at all — writing `x\uD800y` as the bytes `78 ef bf bd 79`, i.e. U+FFFD. So a surrogate path skipped the fail-safe entirely and got a copy-pasteable `mv` naming a replacement-character sibling rather than the real database. Corrected rule, and the transferable one: derive the guard from the consumer only when that consumer is the ONLY thing between you and the reader — otherwise the predicate has to be the UNION over every transform on the path out, and the way to find the others is to ask what encodes, not just what sanitizes. Reachability was checked rather than argued and is worth recording because it cuts the other way from the fix: a lone surrogate CANNOT arrive through `TN_DB_PATH`, since an environment variable is decoded from UTF-8 bytes and that round trip already replaces it — the only route is a direct `runMigrations()` call from JS. Fixed anyway, because the cost was one character class in a shared constant and the alternative is a guard correct only for the inputs someone happened to think of. Mutation-pinned: dropping `\p{Cs}` from the class reddens the new regression and nothing else
- 2026-07-31 · process · (#46, PR #52 — caught by CI, not by the local suite or eight rounds of review) the lone-surrogate regression written for round 7 passed on macOS and FAILED ON LINUX CI with `caught` undefined: `runMigrations` simply never threw there. The test seeded a database at the U+FFFD-encoded filename and then called with the lone-surrogate STRING, on the reasoning that both encode to the same UTF-8 bytes and therefore name the same file — true of this machine, not true of the CI runner. What it actually asserted was a FILESYSTEM'S NAME-RESOLUTION COINCIDENCE rather than the module's behavior, and that is invisible on the machine where the coincidence holds. The root cause is more general than one test: a lone surrogate cannot exist as a real filename on ANY platform, because every OS stores path bytes and encoding one to UTF-8 already replaces it — so an END-TO-END test of that input was never well-defined in the first place, and reaching for one was the mistake. Fixed by exercising the pure functions (`UNRENDERABLE`, `losslessPath`) directly, exported with the reason stated at the export, and leaving end-to-end coverage to the NEWLINE case, which is a legal filename everywhere and passed on both platforms throughout. Two rules. When an input cannot exist in the real world the test is modelling, test the function and not the world — an end-to-end test of an impossible input asserts the harness, not the code. And a green local suite says nothing about platform-dependent behavior: this survived a local run, three consecutive full runs, and EIGHT adversarial review rounds including one that specifically examined this test, because every one of those ran on the same OS
- 2026-07-31 · process · (#46, PR #52) the migration-number collision :180 documented from #17 happened AGAIN, and the recorded resolution held exactly: #49 landed 0006/0007/0008 on `main` while this branch carried its own 0006, so the resolution was to DELETE mine, take main's `drizzle/` wholesale from the index stages, and re-run `drizzle-kit generate` against the merged schema — never hand-edit a snapshot. Mine renumbered to 0009 cleanly. What :180 did not record, and what cost the most attention this time, is the SECOND-ORDER work a renumber creates: every reference to the old number is now a reference to SOMEBODY ELSE'S migration. Here that was six source/doc files plus the user-facing `.pre-0006.bak` backup suffix — and, subtlest, `buildLegacyMigrationsFolder(5)`, which encoded "everything before MY migration" as a literal 5 and silently became wrong when that answer turned into 8. Recover..." — the database orphaned after a full stop, in the one sentence a human reads while their migration is broken. It survived a Codex round that specifically reviewed this function, and my own reading of the same diff, because reading source does not render it: the defect exists only in the concatenation's OUTPUT. Caught by running `tn db migrate` against a seeded duplicate-pair database as a post-merge check, which is the same practice :180 prescribes for behavioral merge hazards and which keeps paying out for a different reason each time. - 2026-07-31 · note · (#46, PR #52, Codex adversarial review round 10, rated high, ACCEPTED AS MECHANISM and DOCUMENTED rather than fixed) renumbering this branch's migration 0006 to 0009 after #49 landed leaves one broken upgrade path, and the mechanism is worth knowing independently of this PR: drizzle's sqlite migrator applies a migration only `if (Number(lastDbMigration.created_at) < migration.folderMillis)` (`sqlite-core/dialect.js`) — a single TIMESTAMP WATERMARK, not a per-migration hash check. So a database migrated on this branch BEFORE the renumber recorded the old 0006's `when` (1785511662427), which happens to sit BETWEEN #49's 0006 (1785511384473) and its 0007 (1785515827922). The consequence is not the obvious one: 0007, 0008 and 0009 all still apply, only #49's 0006 is silently SKIPPED, and 0009 then fails because the index it creates already exists — leaving the roster-retirement columns missing AND a failed migrate. A renumber is therefore not a rename: it moves your migration's position relative to every OTHER branch's timestamps, and any migration whose `when` falls under a stale watermark is skipped without comment. Severity turns on reachability, checked rather than argued (the practice :181 established for the structurally identical #17 PR A round 6 window): a full-filesystem search found nadal databases ONLY in /tmp scratch directories from verification runs — none in any `data/`, which is gitignored — and the branch is unmerged, so the window closes PERMANENTLY at merge since every database created afterwards applies 0000..0009 in order against one consistent journal. Documented in `docs/runbooks/db-migration-recovery.md` with the same one-line recovery as the `is_home` window it sits beside, rather than carrying migration-reconciliation machinery in production forever for a state with no instance on a database the spec makes a disposable cache
- 2026-07-31 · bug · (#18, PR #54 round 5, Codex adversarial review, rated CRITICAL under PROJECT.md's Review Severity Framework — raised from the reviewer's own High, "security hole" ⇒ Critical) `match_add`'s `sourceImage` was validated only as a non-empty string, then `readFileSync`'d and copied into `raw/scorecard/` by `archiveScorecardImage` — BEFORE `addMatchFromScorecard` checked a single thing about the rest of the payload. A syntactically valid payload naming `sourceImage: "/Users/x/.ssh/id_rsa"` or `"/etc/hosts"` persisted those bytes and THEN returned a refusal: `match_add`/`tn match add` was an arbitrary local-file-read-and-copy primitive reachable by anything that could reach the MCP server, gated by nothing PR #48's fd-anchored destination hardening ever touched (that hardening constrains where the archive WRITES, not what it READS). Fixed with four independent checks, all in `archiveScorecardImage` (`src/ingest/match-add.ts`), each refusing before the next runs: (1) containment — `sourceImage` must resolve (real path, no symlink anywhere in the chain, checked via the new `assertInputPathSafe` in `src/fs/output-root.ts`, the READ-side counterpart to the existing write-side `assertOutputPathSafe`) inside a newly-configured root, `TN_SCORECARD_PHOTOS_PATH` / `scorecard-photos/` by default, mirroring the `TN_DB_PATH`/`TN_RAW_PATH`/`TN_REPORTS_PATH` idiom exactly; (2) size — `statSync` before any read, refusing anything over 25 MiB without ever loading it into memory; (3) extension allow-list (`.jpg`/`.jpeg`/`.png`); (4) content — a magic-byte sniff of the bytes actually read, independent of what the extension claims, so a non-image file renamed `.png` still refuses. Separately, and just as load-bearing: archiving used to run unconditionally whenever `sourceImage` was present, so a REFUSED ingest (unknown team, unresolved player, anything) still persisted the photo. The new `addMatchFromScorecardWithArchive` (called by both `tn match add` and `match_add` — the one shared orchestration point, matching how `archiveScorecardImage` itself was already shared) runs the DB write FIRST and archives ONLY after it succeeds, so a refusal now persists nothing. Tests: outside-root, symlinked, non-image-content, and oversized sources are each refused (unit level); an unknown-team and an unresolved-player refusal each leave `raw/scorecard/` untouched even with an otherwise-valid `sourceImage` (service level, and the unknown-team case again end-to-end through the CLI); the happy path still archives correctly with its provenance sidecar (regression guard, both levels)
- 2026-07-31 · decision · (#18, PR #54 round 5) reordering archiving to run AFTER the DB write creates an interaction Codex explicitly asked to be resolved deliberately rather than left implicit: the write can no longer share ONE failure boundary with the archive step, because by the time archiving runs the write has ALREADY COMMITTED — a bad path, an oversized file, anything, and there is no transaction left to roll back into. Chosen: `addMatchFromScorecardWithArchive` returns the service result EXACTLY as `addMatchFromScorecard` produced it (including `ok: true`) plus an optional `archiveError`, never swallowed and never thrown as an opaque error that would hide a successful `teamMatchId` behind what reads as total failure. The CLI surfaces this as `status=partial` (exit 1, but every successful field — `teamMatchId` included — still printed), reusing the EXACT precedent `tn team pull` already set for "the write committed, a requested detail did not" (`skippedRosterEntries`, `docs/findings.md` — the same status string, the same reasoning, not a new invention); the MCP tool returns `archiveError` as a plain field alongside a normal successful return, matching how `team_pull`'s MCP handler already surfaces `skippedRosterEntries` without throwing. A caller that fixes the path (or omits `sourceImage`) and re-runs the identical payload is safe either way, since the ingest is idempotent on `(playedOn, the team pair, slot)`
- 2026-07-31 · note · (#18, PR #54 round 5, stated limitations rather than silent gaps) three things this fix deliberately does NOT close, alongside the naming choice that avoids a real collision: (1) `assertInputPathSafe` is NOT fd-anchored the way the write side's `openNewOutputFileSafely` is — it checks the path, then the caller reads it in a separate step, leaving a narrow check-then-use window a sophisticated local attacker racing the filesystem could in principle exploit; closing it would need the same open-then-verify machinery the write side already has, not yet built for reads. (2) Only JPEG and PNG sniff as valid — HEIC (many phones' default capture format) and WEBP are not supported yet; a real in-the-field HEIC photo will refuse until re-saved or converted. (3) The new root is named `TN_SCORECARD_PHOTOS_PATH` / `scorecard-photos/`, deliberately NOT `TN_SCORECARD_INTAKE_PATH` / `intake/` — this repo already has an unrelated "Intake Pipeline" (Watchlist voices, `scout`/`clip`, AGENTS.md → *Skills*), and reusing that word for a second, different meaning right next to it would be exactly the kind of same-word-different-meaning collision worth avoiding on sight rather than discovering later

A review that crashes is not a review that found nothing; read the log before discarding it. Also worth noting the log showed an early `{"verdict":"approve","findings":[]}` interim message minutes before the real analysis — an approve captured mid-stream is not a verdict, which is a good reason never to parse the first verdict-shaped object out of a job log

The resolution was not a new mechanism but noticing the symmetry I had missed: on a planless run there is no approved plan for ANYTHING in the diff, so `verify` has no plan-alignment baseline for the FIX either — the findings line is exactly as sanctioned as the change it rides with, and no more, because the HC's decision to compress is the authorization for the whole diff. Concrete interleaving: `mid=181505` stored with `eventId=null`; connection A pre-selects (sees null); connection B ALSO pre-selects (sees the SAME null, since A has not written yet) and commits event 102; A then commits event 101 from its now-stale pre-selected null — neither call throws, and B's event is silently overwritten. Fixed by collapsing the conflict check and the write into ONE atomic statement: `onConflictDoUpdate` now carries a `setWhere` gating the whole update on "stored event is null, OR incoming is null, OR they already match" (the only conditions under which writing is safe), with `eventId` computed via SQL `coalesce` on the stored column rather than a JS expression; when none of those hold, SQLite's own UPSERT semantics skip the update entirely, so `.returning().get()` comes back `undefined` — the sole signal used to throw the conflict error (a fresh insert or a permitted update always returns a row). This deliberately makes the two `upsertTeamMatch` branches use DIFFERENT mechanisms for the identical notion (preserve/fill/refuse) after round 8 explicitly asked for one shared mechanism — correctness across connections requires the check-and-write to be one SQL round-trip, which only a real conflict-target column (the id-less branch has none) can support this way; the divergence is documented in the code as deliberate, not drift. Testing note: a literal interleaved race cannot be expressed as a black-box red/green test against `upsertTeamMatch` — its own pre-select was already always fresh at call time, so the vulnerability lived entirely INSIDE one call's read-then-write gap, which synchronous JS test code cannot pause mid-execution to exploit; confirmed by running both new tests against the round-8 code before touching anything and finding them already green. Verified instead with two tests: a CHARACTERIZATION test using raw table operations to reconstruct the exact two-connection sequence team-lead described, proving the underlying hazard is real independent of any one function's implementation; and a cross-connection correctness test on the real, fixed function.

NOT fixed here, and not cheap the way the source-id branch's fix was: that fix worked because `sourceMatchId` is a REAL, DB-unique conflict target `onConflictDoUpdate` can gate a `setWhere` on; the id-less branch's identity (unordered team pair + `playedOn` + NORMALIZED time/site) has no backing unique index at all — by design, since the normalization can't be expressed as a plain column constraint — so there is no equivalent atomic-UPSERT mechanism available for it without inventing one (a stored, indexed normalized-key column plus a schema migration, well past this round's scope). The one CHEAP lever that WOULD close it — switching both callers' `db.transaction(...)` calls to `{ behavior: "immediate" }`, forcing the write lock at BEGIN rather than at the first write — is named here rather than applied unilaterally, because it changes the locking/serialization behavior of the WHOLE containing transaction in both pipelines, not just this one race, which is a broader trade-off than this round's narrow fix and deserves an explicit go-ahead rather than a silent widening.

The transferable rule is about WHERE a capability should live, not about how carefully to build it: an error string that hands a human something EXECUTABLE has to be correct against the shell, the filesystem, the encoder and the platform simultaneously, and each of those is a moving target owned by someone else — whereas the same command in a runbook is correct by construction because markdown is not a consumer that transforms it. - 2026-07-31 · process · (#18, PR #54, spanning rounds 5-6 — the most transferable lesson of the run) a SECURITY residual was written down accurately and shipped anyway, and the sentence that recorded it turned out to describe the vulnerability rather than a footnote to it. Round 5's fix for the `sourceImage` arbitrary-file-read logged, in the code and in this file, that `assertInputPathSafe` is "NOT fd-anchored like the write side — a narrower check-then-use window". Every word true. Round 6 then showed that window WAS the hole: a hard link inside the configured root satisfies containment, symlink refusal, size and magic-byte checks simultaneously, because all four describe a PATHNAME while the thing read is a FILE. The orchestrator (me) read that residual, accepted it, and approved the commit. Two rules worth keeping, and they are different from the usual "don't overclaim" lesson this log already records many times. First: RECORDING a limitation and EVALUATING it are separate acts, and for a security control the second is not optional — the question a residual must answer before it ships is not "is this sentence accurate?" but "what does an attacker do with exactly this?", which is one adversarial minute and was never spent. Second, the sharper one: the fix was the wrong CLASS of check, not an incomplete one, and the tell was available statically — every validation named a NAME while the operation consumed a FILE. When a control validates one kind of thing and the protected operation consumes another, no amount of adding checks of the first kind closes it; that mismatch is the thing to look for, not the count of checks. Compounding it: the correct technique was already in this repo — PR #48's `openNewOutputFileSafely` does post-open symlink/inode/link-count re-verification on the WRITE side — and was not carried across to the read side, so the fix existed in the codebase, one file away, while the hole shipped

A marker that appears in the prose describing it cannot be found by naive substring search; the scan needs a stricter shape (line-anchored heading) or manual disambiguation, and a run that trusted the count would have transcribed its own plan text as a finding.
- 2026-07-31 · process · (#19) in a git worktree, `git checkout main` fails with "already used by worktree", so the reflexive branch-from-main sequence silently does not run; `git checkout -b <branch>` then bases the new branch on whatever the worktree had checked out — here a *merged* feature-branch tip four commits behind `origin/main`. Caught by checking `git rev-parse HEAD` against `origin/main` before any work. The worktree-safe form is `git checkout -b <branch> && git fetch origin main && git reset --hard origin/main`.

A runbook naming a command, flag, or positional shape that does not exist has zero automated detection — which is why this phase's dry run was built as an executable test pinning the command spine rather than as a prose walkthrough.

- 2026-07-31 · process · (#19) `sqlite3`'s dot-commands (`.backup`/`.restore`) run their OWN quote-aware tokenizer over the one string-argument they receive, independent of the outer shell's — so `sqlite3 "$DB" ".backup '$path'"` where `$path` contains an apostrophe (`O'Brien.bak`) does not fail cleanly: the apostrophe closes the inner single quote early, `.backup`'s two-argument form (`?DB? FILE`) reads the fragment before the break as an attach-point name, and the LEFTOVER fragment (`Brien.bak'`, unquoted, not absolute) got silently written as a zero-byte file in the shell's own cwd — which in one verification run was this repo's root, caught only by `git status` before commit. Double-quoting the inner argument survives an apostrophe; a broken-quote failure in a tool that opens files should always be followed by a stray-file check, not just a stderr read.

The scoping instinct was wrong twice over: PROJECT.md → *Findings-Log Discipline* asks "is something broken?" FIRST and answers "defects get fixed — in the running PR when the fix is in hand", and the phase's own plan carried a standing rule saying the same. Neither is suspended by a task being labelled docs-only; a task's label describes what it set out to do, not what it is allowed to fix when it trips over something broken. Worth noticing that the defect was found by a DOCUMENTATION task: writing the runbook meant printing a real binder and reading it, which is exactly the thing no test was doing, and the false sentence had survived every unit test because each one asserted on the section EXISTING rather than on what it said.
- 2026-07-31 · process · (#19, PR #58 round 1, Codex adversarial review, rated High) a runbook that tells an operator to paste a stored value into a quoted command template (`tn team pull "<team name>"`) is the SAME defect class as an error message that emits a shell command (the #56/#57 lesson at :292), arriving from the opposite direction — and the earlier lesson's own framing hid it. That entry concluded "prefer diagnosis-plus-pointer to instruction-plus-payload" and named the runbook as the SAFE destination, because markdown does not transform what it carries. True, and beside the point here: the hazard was never that markdown mangles the payload, it is that a HUMAN following the instruction performs the interpolation, and the shell then parses the result. A team name is scraped data (`A"; touch /tmp/pwn; #` closes the quote and runs `touch`), and the very step above it printed those names raw out of the database. The fix is not better escaping advice — it is to never hand over a template to interpolate into: bind the value to a shell variable and pass `"$team"`, which is expanded as one argument and never re-parsed as syntax, or read it in a `while IFS= read -r` loop. Residual, stated in the runbook rather than papered over: a name containing a literal newline splits into two loop records, which fails CLOSED (an unresolved target, nothing written) rather than executing anything.
- 2026-07-31 · process · (#19, PR #58 round 1, Codex adversarial review, rated Medium) `sqlite3 <path> <query>` CREATES an empty database when `<path>` does not exist, so a read-only-looking enumeration step converts a `TN_DB_PATH` typo into an apparently-valid empty answer: the query prints no rows, the operator reads "nothing to refresh", and a decoy file is left behind that a later `tn db migrate` migrates without complaint. An open-creates-a-file API makes a *read* mutate, so any documented read against a user-supplied database path needs `[ -f "$DB" ]` before it — and the same behavior is what makes a failed `.backup` verifiable-looking, since verifying a backup that was never written opens an empty database whose `PRAGMA integrity_check` answers `ok`.
- 2026-07-31 · process · (#19, PR #58 round 1, Codex adversarial review, rated Medium — the sharpest finding of the round) a synthesized fixture whose optional section is EMPTY makes "the parser correctly found nothing" and "the parser is broken" indistinguishable, and a test built on one cannot tell them apart. `buildRosterPage` emitted a header-only local-schedule table, so gutting `parseSchedule` to `return []` left every assertion in a test that advertises itself as a WHOLE-PIPELINE dry run still passing — while a live pull would have silently dropped every fixture. Proven both ways rather than argued: the induced defect passed before the fix and fails after it. The transferable rule is about what a fixture must contain, not about adding another assertion: a pipeline test's fixture has to carry at least one real row of every structure the pipeline claims to parse, because a structure with zero rows exercises the absence path only, and the absence path is exactly the one a broken parser also produces. Worth noting the fixture was header-only for a GOOD local reason (a real schedule row mints an opponent team, which would have polluted the roster assertions) — the resolution was to name the opponent that already exists, not to accept the blind spot.
- 2026-07-31 · process · (#19, PR #58 round 1) `sqlite3`'s dot-commands re-parse their argument with SQLite's OWN tokenizer, a second quoting layer the outer shell's quoting cannot protect — so `.backup "$BAK"` is not safe merely because `$BAK` was read with `IFS= read -r` and quoted. Two layers of quoting means two layers of validation, and the check has to run BEFORE the destructive half: this runbook now refuses a `"` or newline in the path ahead of both `.backup` and `.restore`, having previously only documented that such a path "does not work" — which is a warning printed next to the loaded gun rather than a safety on it. Verified the guard is not over-broad: a legitimate path containing both a space and an apostrophe still passes it and round-trips every row.
- 2026-07-31 · process · (#19, PR #58 round 2, Codex adversarial review, rated High — and it is a correction to MY OWN round-1 fix at :290) the round-1 fix for shell injection in a runbook swapped the quote character and called it solved: `tn team pull "<name>"` became `team='Paste the name between these single quotes'`. An apostrophe closes a single quote exactly as a double quote closes a double quote, and apostrophes in team names are ordinary rather than exotic — so `x'; touch /tmp/pwn; #` executed during the ASSIGNMENT, before `"$team"` was ever expanded. Confirmed by running it. The lesson is not "use better quoting": **the template itself is the defect**, and any instruction whose final step is "splice this text into a command" is unfixable by choosing different delimiters, because the operator performs the splice and the shell parses the result. Two forms actually fix it, and both remove the interpolation rather than escaping it — read the value at a prompt (`printf …; IFS= read -r team`, what this repo's other runbooks already do, and what nothing in the shell ever re-parses), or carry a transport that cannot hold shell syntax at all (here: each team's stored `tennisrecord_url`, which `tn team pull` accepts directly — percent-encoding means no space, quote or newline can appear in it). Recording it because round 1 FELT like a fix, was written up confidently as one, and shipped the same class of bug one character to the left.
- 2026-07-31 · process · (#19, PR #58 round 2, Codex adversarial review, rated High) round 1 documented a residual — "a name containing a newline splits into two loop records, which fails CLOSED: both halves are unresolved, nothing is written" — and that sentence was simply **false**, asserted from reasoning and never executed. If a team is stored as `North⏎South` while teams named `North` and `South` also exist (ordinary, not contrived), each half resolves EXACTLY against a real unrelated team and live-pulls it, updating and potentially retiring rosters nobody asked to touch. This is the same shape as the security-residual lesson at :294 and it is worth the second entry precisely because that one was already written down: recording a limitation and EVALUATING it are separate acts, and the second is where a "known limit" is either confirmed or exposed as the bug. The tell was available without any tooling — the claim was about what `tn` does with a half-name, and nothing in the run ever ran `tn` with a half-name. A residual stated in a runbook is a testable claim; if it is not worth testing it is not worth asserting.
- 2026-07-31 · process · (#19, PR #58 round 2, Codex adversarial review, rated High) `sqlite3 "$DB" "select …" | while IFS= read -r x; do …; done` reports **the status of the loop**, never the producer's — so an unreadable or corrupt database emits zero rows, the loop body never runs, the pipeline exits 0, and a total enumeration failure is indistinguishable from "there was nothing to do". The same construct hides per-item failure: with items A then B, a failed A followed by a successful B leaves the loop's status 0, so a partial refresh reads as a clean one. Both confirmed by running them. Three changes fix it together and none is sufficient alone: enumerate into a file and check the producer's own exit status; drive the loop with `< file` (a redirect, so it runs in THIS shell) rather than a pipe (a subshell); and accumulate an explicit `failed` flag inside it, because a loop's exit status is only its last iteration's. Generalizes past sqlite3 to every `producer | while read` in any runbook or script.
- 2026-07-31 · process · (#19, PR #58 round 2, Codex adversarial review, rated Medium) a guard was added to the enumeration snippet and **not** to the separately-pasteable backup block, so `.backup` still opened a nonexistent source database — SQLite creates one, `.backup` faithfully copies it, and the result is a real, structurally valid, EMPTY backup whose `PRAGMA integrity_check` answers `ok`. The transferable rule is about runbook structure rather than about sqlite3: **a runbook's unit of use is the fenced block, not the document**, because an operator pastes one block at a time and blocks get run out of order, re-run alone, or copied into a note. Every precondition a block depends on has to live IN that block, even when that means the same two lines appear four times — "it was checked earlier in the file" is an assumption about reading order that the artifact's own format invites breaking.
- 2026-07-31 · process · (#19, PR #58 round 3, Codex adversarial review, rated High) `[ "$failed" -eq 0 ] || echo "STOP: …" >&2` ends the block with **echo's** exit status, which is 0 — so the accumulator that existed specifically to stop a partial refresh reading as a clean one still let the block exit 0, and the prose asserted the opposite. A `cond || action` idiom reports the ACTION's status whenever the condition is false, which inverts its meaning at exactly the moment it fires; `if cond; then …; exit 1; fi` is the form that actually fails. Three rounds in a row now, the same shape: a guard was added, the guard's own exit path was not checked, and the runbook confidently described a behavior nobody had run. Round 1 added the accumulator, round 2 hardened the loop around it, round 3 found the accumulator never mattered.
- 2026-07-31 · process · (#19, PR #58 round 3, Codex adversarial review, rated High) the "a runbook's unit of use is the fenced BLOCK, not the document" rule written into this log one round earlier at :296 was violated in the same file that motivated it: the guard went into step 2's first fence and not its separately-pasteable readback fence, so `sqlite3 "$BAK" "select count(*)…"` on a missing path still CREATED an empty database — which then satisfied step 3's `[ -f "$BAK" ]` and could be restored over a live database. Writing a rule down does not apply it to the artifact retroactively; the round that states a rule should re-audit every existing instance of the pattern in the same pass, not just the one that triggered it. Also fixed properly rather than only guarded: verification reads now open `file:$BAK?mode=ro`, so a wrong path cannot create anything even if a guard is someday missed again — a read that cannot write is stronger than a check that a file exists.
- 2026-07-31 · bug · (#19, PR #58 round 3, Codex adversarial review, rated High) `resolveTargetUrl` (`src/ingest/team-pull.ts:48-53`, and its twin in `player-pull.ts`) accepts ANY `http://`/`https://` target with no host validation, and `pullTeam` then persists that exact string as `teams.tennisrecord_url` (:146). One operator-chosen pull against another host therefore leaves that host in the column permanently — and a bulk refresh that enumerates the column re-fetches it on EVERY run from then on, letting that page rewrite the roster. The distinction that matters is not "can the operator target an arbitrary URL" (they can, deliberately, and a human is in the loop) but "does a one-time target silently become a standing automatic one" — which is what an unfiltered batch converts it into. Closed at the artifact level in this PR: the runbook's batch selects only `tennisrecord.com` hosts and PRINTS anything it skipped rather than dropping it silently. NOT fixed in `src/`: adding a host allow-list changes the ingest contract for `team pull`, `player pull` and the `tr:` prefix together, and whether nadal should refuse a non-TennisRecord host outright, warn, or make it configurable is a product decision with real blast radius rather than an obvious repair — raised to the HC rather than decided unilaterally inside a Phase-7 verification PR.
- 2026-07-31 · process · (#19, PR #58 round 4, Codex adversarial review, rated High) fixing an unsafe pattern in the file that motivated it, while leaving four sibling files demonstrating the same pattern, makes the PR contradict itself: `pre-tournament-full-pull.md` said "never paste a scraped team name between quotes" while `build-and-print-dossiers.md` — edited in the same PR — opened with `tn team pull "<team>" --players` as its first instruction, and three more runbooks did the same. A newly-written rule is a claim about the whole artifact set, not about the file that happens to state it, so the round that introduces one owes a sweep of every sibling for the same shape. This is the docs-sweep-by-concept lesson already in this log arriving with teeth: the concept was "an instruction whose final step is splicing untrusted text into a command", and grepping the CONCEPT (`pull "<`, `home "<`, `show "<`) found every instance in seconds. Also worth keeping: the sweep distinguished *executable* sites (a fenced block or a precondition bullet an operator runs) from *descriptive* prose naming a command form, and only the former were rewritten — a rule applied mechanically to prose would have made four files worse.
- 2026-07-31 · process · (#19, PR #58 round 4, Codex adversarial review, rated Medium — and it is a defect INTRODUCED by round 3's own fix) `file:$BAK?mode=ro`, adopted in round 3 to make a verification read incapable of creating anything, added a THIRD parser between the operator and the file: `?` starts the URI's query string, `#` a fragment, `%` an escape. So a perfectly legal backup path `/tmp/good?x=1` becomes `file:/tmp/good?x=1?mode=ro`, which opens `/tmp/good` — a DIFFERENT database — with `mode=ro` silently dropped, and the runbook then certifies row counts from the wrong file before restoring the unverified backup. Confirmed by building both files and watching the read return the decoy's rows. The shape worth remembering: a fix that adds a layer adds that layer's parser, and "this is read-only now" was reasoned about as a permission change when it was also a syntax change. The repair refuses `?`/`#`/`%` alongside the `"` and newline the dot-command already refused — one refusal covering all three parsers, rather than three escaping strategies — because the set of characters a path may contain is a far smaller thing to get right than the composition of three quoting rules.
- 2026-07-31 · process · (#19, PR #58 round 4, Codex adversarial review, rated Low) a cleanup that runs only on the happy path is not cleanup: `urls="$(mktemp)"` followed by a loop of network pulls leaked the file — which lists every refresh target — on Ctrl-C or session termination, because the `rm -f` sat after the loop and the error branches, and a signal reaches neither. `trap 'rm -f "$urls"' EXIT HUP INT TERM` immediately after the `mktemp` is the fix, and EXIT alone is not enough for an interactive block an operator is likely to interrupt mid-run. Verified by interrupting a real script and confirming the file was gone.
- 2026-07-31 · process · (#19, PR #58 round 5, Codex adversarial review, rated Medium) a signal trap that only CLEANS UP does not cancel anything: in POSIX sh the handler runs and control returns to the next command, so `trap 'rm -f "$urls"' EXIT HUP INT TERM` around a loop of network pulls meant Ctrl-C deleted the temp file and then calmly kept issuing the remaining pulls. Proven by running both forms against SIGTERM — the cleanup-only trap logged `CLEANED CONTINUED CLEANED`, the split form logged `ABORTED CLEANED`. The fix is two traps with different jobs: `trap cleanup EXIT` (which also preserves an already-set nonzero status) plus `trap 'cleanup; exit 130' HUP INT TERM`. Methodology note worth as much as the finding: the first attempt to test this appeared to show BOTH forms continuing, because a background job started by a non-interactive shell has SIGINT set to ignore — the test was wrong, not the fix, and `kill -TERM` showed the real difference. A negative result from a harness that cannot deliver the stimulus is not evidence.
- 2026-07-31 · process · (#19, PR #58 round 5, Codex adversarial review, rated Medium) the round-4 sweep for unsafe runbook shell searched for the paste-into-quotes SHAPE and therefore missed a different hazard in the same files: `in-event-screenshot-ingest.md`'s verification step hardcoded `sqlite3 data/nadal.db …`, ignoring `TN_DB_PATH` entirely, so it read a stale database whenever one was configured — and created a decoy when that default path held nothing. A readback that silently inspects a DIFFERENT database than the write it is verifying is worse than no readback, since it manufactures confirmation. Two rules fall out: a sweep should enumerate every executable site of a KIND (here: every `sqlite3` invocation in `docs/runbooks/`) rather than every instance of the one pattern that prompted it, and any documented command that reads application state must resolve that state the same way the application does — `${TN_DB_PATH:-data/nadal.db}`, matching `dbPath()`, never a hardcoded default. The same step also interpolated a pasted `teamMatchId` straight into SQL; it is now checked numeric first, on the principle that a readback is the last place a value should reach a query unvalidated.
- 2026-07-31 · process · (#19, PR #58 round 5, Codex adversarial review, rated Medium) `mode=ro` was documented as meaning the read "cannot create or modify anything", which overclaims: SQLite's WAL documentation permits a read-only connection to create `-wal`/`-shm` sidecars when they are absent and the directory is writable, so the guarantee is about the DATABASE's contents, not about the filesystem. For a finished, quiescent backup the stronger and more accurate form is `mode=ro&immutable=1`, now used for both backup verifications; the live-database reads keep plain `mode=ro` (immutable would be a lie about a file `tn` may be writing) with the claim corrected to say what it actually guarantees. Honest limit on the verification: attempting to reproduce sidecar creation under `mode=ro` on this machine produced none, so this rests on the documented behavior rather than an observed failure — which is precisely why the fix was to stop overclaiming rather than to assert a new guarantee I had not demonstrated either.

The supportable claim is much narrower — the print CSS is *inlined*, so a preview needs no network or external stylesheet. Worth generalizing carefully — a pinning test is green the moment it is written and therefore cannot be TDD'd, so its failure mode has to be *observed by mutation* or it pins nothing; asserting that it would fail is not the same as watching it fail

- 2026-08-01 · process · closed · (#36, PR #61, Codex adversarial review, rated Medium) a test that pins a **decision not to build something** must assert the OUTCOME, not the rejection mechanism: asserting only `exit 1` + `unrecognized flag --pdf` would still pass if a future change wrote `reports/index.pdf` on seeing the flag and *then* fell through to the same parser error, so the test proved "this flag is not declared" while its own comment claimed it proved "no PDF is produced". The general shape is the standing one — assert behaviour, not the path that happens to implement it — but the absence-pinning case is where it is easiest to miss, because the mechanism assertion *feels* like the whole point when the feature does not exist. Page chrome also renders the signed-in user's own name and a 10-digit `USTA Account #` on every page. That last one is the redaction-relevant part: it is the *capturing operator's* identity, not the subject's, so it is exactly the class a substitution map built from scouting targets would omit.

- 2026-08-01 · process · closed · (#27, ship run, Task 1) driving an authenticated legacy WebForms site from an automation harness failed three independent ways, and the lesson is to stop earlier than I did: (a) the team/player links are `javascript:` hrefs, so synthetic clicks navigate nowhere; (b) setting `<select>` values then firing `__doPostBack` lost sibling field values, because the postback re-renders from server state rather than from the DOM I had just mutated; (c) the browser extension blocks any tool result that looks like bulk query-string data, so hrefs cannot be harvested to reconstruct URLs. The workaround that *did* work for (c) is worth keeping: extracting only the **parameter names** (`([A-Za-z_]\w*)=` over `outerHTML`) passes the guard and is what a detector set actually needs, so the block cost nothing once the query stopped asking for values. The general shape: when three distinct approaches to the same mechanical step fail, the step is the wrong one — a human already at the keyboard navigates a hostile UI in seconds, and the runbook design (HC drives, tool captures) was right the whole time.
- 2026-08-01 · bug · closed · (#27, found while capturing a live signed-in TennisLink page; a fix was attempted across four review rounds and WITHDRAWN — tracked at #80) `NEVER_PUBLISH` in `tools/redact-fixture.ts` enumerated third-party PII — email, mailto, tel, phone, street address — and had **no credential class**, but a page captured from a logged-in session carries the *capturing operator's* secrets too: a real TennisLink league page ships a 172-character `hdnCSRFToken` and a **14,420-character `__VIEWSTATE`** inline. Nothing had leaked (every committed fixture greps clean, because USTA's profile is a client-rendered SPA and TennisRecord is public — the exposure needed WebForms to arrive), so this was latent rather than shipped. The dangerous part was not the leak path but the *refusal* path: the allow-list correctly refuses a 172-char token, and the documented way to clear a refusal is to classify the atom and **add it to the committed vocabulary** — and an opaque 172-character string reads as "boilerplate" to someone working that loop mechanically. So correct adherence to the written procedure could commit a live session token to a public repo. Fix: empty credential-field values before any sweep (lossless — no parser reads a hidden WebForms field, and the element plus every other attribute survives), with an exact-equality backstop assertion. Emptying rather than substituting is what keeps it out of the refusal report at all, since an empty value reduces to an empty skeleton. The class worth carrying forward: a control that fails *closed* can still fail *badly*, when the documented remedy for the refusal is the harmful act.

The synthetic test fixture I wrote from the diagnosis contained no such element, so the unit tests were green while the behaviour was wrong on the only page that mattered. Fix: skip `type=submit|button|reset|image`, whose `value` is a label by definition. This is the *fixes-authored-with-the-diagnosis-are-least-reviewed* shape again, and the thing that caught it was cheap and repeatable — run the actual matcher over the actual page and report counts, which needs no secret to enter the transcript.
