A source-level marginal-value question belongs in Assess, before the options are drawn — the plan-revision that followed was cheap only because nothing had been committed yet.
- 2026-08-01 · bug · closed · (#27, PR #79, Stage-4 adversarial pass, fail-open lens — a defect in this run's OWN fix, found before any reviewer saw it) the credential sweep anchored its attribute patterns with `\b`, and `\b` matches after a hyphen: `\btype=` therefore also matches `data-type=`, and `\bname=` also matches `data-name=`. One direction fails open — `<input data-type="submit" type="hidden" id="hdnCSRFToken" value="…">` was read as a button label and **skipped**, leaving a live token in place — and the other over-strips a benign field whose `data-name` happens to match. Fixed at the time by anchoring to a real attribute boundary, `(?<![-\w])`, rather than a word boundary — though the control that fix lived in was later withdrawn entirely (#80), so nothing in the tree does this today. Two things generalise: an HTML attribute matcher must anchor on the attribute boundary and never on `\b`, since `data-*` prefixing is ubiquitous; and the same pass replaced a hand-copied duplicate of the value regex inside the backstop assertion with the shared constant, because a backstop that disagrees with the thing it backs up is not a backstop. The lens that found it was *fail-open*, chosen at the summons — a round-count bound would not have asked the question.
- 2026-08-01 · risk · open · (#27, PR #79, Stage-4 adversarial pass, permanent "what class is not on this list?" lens; **the first draft of this entry overstated the gap and was corrected by a later review round — the correction is the more useful half**) TennisLink addresses its league pages as `StatsAndStandings.aspx?SearchType=3#&&s=<33-char token>`, and the entity reference lives only in that token, so it cannot be dropped from a provenance sidecar. I first recorded this as "no layer examines a URL token". **That was wrong**: `capture-fixture.ts` runs `sourceUrl` through `redact()` *with the vocabulary*, so the allow-list atomises it like any other content and an unrecognised token **refuses the capture**. The boundary holds. What is genuinely missing is a *credential-specific classifier* — nothing can say whether that token is an entity key or session state — so the refusal drops the operator into the vocabulary loop, where the tempting move (commit the token to make the refusal go away) is exactly the harmful one. Left `open` because the question "is that token session-bound?" is unanswered and will be reached by whoever captures the first TennisLink fixture; the cheap test is whether the same URL resolves from a different session. The lesson worth more than the finding: **I asserted a gap without checking whether an existing layer already covered it**, which is the same error as claiming a protection that does not exist — overstating a hole erodes trust in the log exactly as overstating a safeguard erodes trust in the code, and both were caught only by a reviewer reading the claim against the source.
- 2026-08-01 · bug · closed · (#27, PR #79, Codex adversarial review — three confirmed defects in my own fix, which triggered PROJECT.md's escalate-on-recurrence rule) hand-written regex over HTML attributes produced a NEW lexical defect every round rather than converging: round 1 `\b` matched after `-` so `data-type` skipped a live token; round 2's `(?<![-\w])` did not cover `:`, so the legal attribute name `data:type` skipped it too; and independently `<(?:input|meta)\b[^>]*>` truncated the tag at a `>` INSIDE a quoted value, so `value="abc>SECRET"` survived untouched — **with the backstop assertion sharing the identical bypass because it reused the same matcher.** Still unexplored at that point: unquoted values, mixed quotes, malformed tags. The escalation rule fired correctly and honoring it was right: the answer was not a fourth delimiter patch but a different design — locate with the real parser (`cheerio` + parse5 `sourceCodeLocationInfo`, giving authoritative `[startIndex, endIndex)` spans and parsed `attribs`), then splice the value inside the parser-supplied span. That closes hyphen, colon and quoted-delimiter simultaneously, because `data-type`/`data:type`/`type` are simply distinct keys and tag boundaries are the tokenizer's problem. **The design was already the house pattern** — `src/parsers/` locates with cheerio, and courtgrab2 before it used Nokogiri `.css()` — this one function had deviated from it. Carry forward: when a tag- or attribute-matching regex produces a SECOND delimiter defect, stop and reach for the parser that is almost certainly already a dependency; and a backstop that reuses the matcher it backs up is not a backstop.
- 2026-08-01 · bug · closed · (#27, PR #79, caught by TDD immediately after the cheerio rework) building a regex with `new RegExp(\`…\${attr}…\`)` inside a **template literal** ate one layer of escaping before the RegExp constructor ever saw it: `\\s` became `s`, so the pattern read `[s/]value s* = s*` and matched nothing. The control silently stripped **zero** fields while `tsc` passed clean and the code read correctly at a glance — a security control that had become a no-op. Fixed by using static regex **literals** keyed by attribute name instead of composing patterns from strings. The general rule: a regex that must survive two layers of escaping (template literal → RegExp) is one layer too many for anything security-bearing; write the literal, close the input set, and let TDD prove it fires. Worth noting the failure mode is *silent and passes review* — nothing about `[s/]value s*` looks wrong unless you execute it.
- 2026-08-01 · bug · closed · (#27, PR #79, Codex adversarial review round 2 against the REWORKED design, rated critical) a parsed attribute map is a **lossy summary of the bytes**, and the credential control trusted it. Given `<input id="hdnCSRFToken" value="public" value="SECRET">` — malformed but browser-tolerated — parse5 keeps only the FIRST `value` and discards the second, so reading `attribs.value` emptied the decoy and left the real token in the file; the backstop then **agreed the output was clean, because it read the same lossy view**. Same on `<meta content="ok" content="SECRET2">`. Fixed by making the writer patterns global (empty every occurrence in the span) and, more importantly, by having the assertion scan the span's **raw bytes** instead of `attribs`. The rule this encodes and the reason it is worth more than the individual fix: **use the parser to DELIMIT, read the BYTES to decide** — anything that will be written to disk must be checked as written, not as parsed. Note this is a genuinely different class from the three delimiter defects that preceded it (those were the tag matcher being lexically wrong; here the parser was correct and the gap was between its normalized view and the file), which is why it warranted a fix rather than a second escalation. Also worth keeping: the regression test asserts the backstop rejects the ORIGINAL input, not merely that it accepts the cleaned output — the weaker assertion passes for a backstop that does nothing.
- 2026-08-01 · bug · closed · (#27, PR #79, Codex adversarial review round 3, rated critical — and it is the SAME CLASS as round 2's, which is what finally settled the design) an unquoted attribute value containing `=` bypassed both stripper and backstop: `<input type=hidden id=hdnCSRFToken value=x=98765432101234567890>`. The HTML spec forbids `=` in an unquoted value, but the spec's own *error handling* appends it, so parse5 read the whole `x=9876…` while my spec-shaped character class stopped at `x` — producing `value=""=98765432101234567890`, a dangling token tail that the backstop then certified as clean. Round 2 was the same shape (parse5 keeps the first of duplicate attributes; my pattern read the other), so this was the second consecutive round in which **a hand-written byte pattern disagreed with the parser about what an attribute value IS**. Patching the character class would have been a third instance. The class only closes by deleting the byte pattern, so the tag was **rebuilt from the parser's attribute map** instead, making duplicates collapse, quoting normalises, and `=`/`>`/mixed quotes/case/self-closing stop being questions this code answers. The cost — a credential tag loses its original formatting — was acceptable precisely and only there, because these are hidden inputs and metas no parser in `src/parsers/` reads. That design is recorded for #80 rather than shipped: round 4 broke it again and the control was withdrawn. Carry forward: when a byte-level pattern and a real parser disagree twice, stop reconciling them and delete the pattern.
- 2026-08-01 · bug · closed · (#27, PR #79, found by sweeping my OWN traces after the round-3 redesign, before any reviewer saw it) the duplicate-attribute class applied to the **exemption** rather than the value, which is where it is most dangerous: `<input type="submit" type="hidden" id="hdnCSRFToken" value="SECRET">` — parse5 keeps the first `type`, so the label-bearing exemption fired and the credential field was skipped **entirely**, token intact. Every other duplicate defense I had added covered `value`/`content`; none covered the attribute that decides whether to look at all. Fixed by refusing the exemption whenever `type` occurs more than once in the raw span — a duplicated attribute means the parsed view cannot be trusted about that element, and an exemption must fail closed. The general lesson is about where to look after fixing a class: I had propagated "duplicates are lossy" to the *values* and stopped, when the same fact invalidated the *guard condition* one line above. A fix for a class is not done until every consumer of the same underlying assumption has been re-checked — including the ones that decide whether the fixed code runs.
- 2026-08-01 · process · closed · (#27, PR #79, after Codex adversarial review round 4 — the decision to WITHDRAW rather than ship) a partial security control that operators trust is worse than an absent one they do not, and that is the argument that settled it. Four rounds produced seven-plus confirmed defects in a credential stripper, and the last three were **one class** (parse5's collapsed attribute map disagreeing with the raw bytes; every raw-span regex written to reconcile them missed a new duplicate form: duplicate `value`, unquoted `value=x=…`, duplicate `id`, bare boolean `type`, `type=` inside a quoted value). PROJECT.md's escalate-on-recurrence rule had already fired once and been overridden by an HC decision to proceed; the second time, the right move was not a fifth patch but removing the feature from the PR. What tipped it was not the defect count but the **claim**: the runbook I had written told operators "session credentials in known fields are handled for you", so shipping with known bypasses would have stopped them looking — strictly worse than the honest "nothing strips these, here is exactly what to grep for" the runbook now says. The reusable shape: when deciding whether to ship a partial control, ask what the DOCUMENTATION will claim and who stops checking because of it, not just what fraction of cases the code covers. Withdrawn to #80 with every trace written up, so the next attempt starts from the failures rather than rediscovering them.
- 2026-08-01 · process · closed · (#27, PR #79, the whole-run shape) this run planned three parsers, delivered none, and its most valuable output was a runbook plus a **withdrawn** feature — yet it was not wasted, and the reason is worth keeping. The `/ship` lifecycle's terminal artifact is a mergeable PR, which creates quiet pressure to land *something* from every stage; what actually protected quality here was that the two governance rules pointed the same way at the end (escalate-on-recurrence said stop patching; `attested` said no merge with open must-fix findings), so "ship less" was the only conforming move. Two lessons. First, the **capture attempt was the valuable part even though it produced no fixture**: everything real in this PR — the URL grammar, the `javascript:`-link obstacle, the credential class, ten findings entries — came from touching the live system, not from planning. Second, **a run that ends by removing its own feature and filing the gap is a successful run**, and the lifecycle should be read that way rather than as a failed delivery; the SOW says so explicitly so a later reader does not mistake #80 for abandoned work.

That is the *exact* false positive the automation had hit and that I had already fixed in code, three days of review rounds earlier; the fix simply did not travel from the code into the prose that replaced the code. Two lessons, and the second is the one worth carrying: (1) a documented procedure needs the same adversarial review as the code it replaces, because prose failure modes are invisible to every check in the repo — this one survived six review rounds precisely because nothing executes it; (2) the moment a fix is *deleted*, every lesson embedded in it becomes homeless, so withdrawing code should trigger an explicit pass over the docs asking "what did that code know that this prose now has to say out loud?" — the button-label exclusion, the value-vs-label distinction, and the attribute scoping were all knowledge the deleted function encoded and the runbook silently dropped.
- 2026-08-01 · bug · closed · (#27, PR #79, Codex adversarial review round 7 — a control-flow deadlock in prose, which no check in this repo can see) the runbook told an operator to scrub session credentials **from the redacted fixture**, at a step after the capture. That fixture does not exist: `tools/capture-fixture.ts` runs the allow-list **before either output file is written**, and the runbook itself says a 172-character token is refused — so on a credential-bearing page the capture produces nothing to edit. An operator following it literally is stranded, and the only exit from that refusal is the single move the same page forbids: adding the token to the vocabulary. Fixed by moving the scrub to a new step **4**, operating on a `.scrubbed` copy of the saved page *outside the repo*, before any capture runs, with an explicit instruction never to re-run the capture against the unscrubbed original. Two things generalise. **A procedure has control flow, and it can deadlock exactly like code — but nothing executes prose, so no test, linter or type-checker will ever catch it; only a reader walking it as an operator will.** And the specific trap here was ordering-by-narrative: "read the fixture before committing it" *reads* like the right final step, which is why the error survived six earlier review rounds that were all checking whether individual sentences were TRUE rather than whether the sequence was EXECUTABLE. Truth-of-claims and executability are different audits and the first does not imply the second.
- 2026-08-01 · bug · closed · (#27, PR #79, Codex adversarial review round 8 — the SAME deadlock as round 7, reopened by an incomplete fix) round 7 moved the credential scrub before the capture and I updated the step's *prose* — "run the capture, against the **scrubbed** copy from step 4" — but left the fenced **command block** reading `--file <the saved page>`. The command is the thing an operator copies, so the deadlock was fully intact for anyone who did what the runbook literally says: the unscrubbed original goes back into the allow-list, the capture refuses, no fixture exists, and the only exit is the forbidden vocabulary workaround. The prose and the command contradicted each other four lines apart. Generalises as: **in a procedure, the executable artifact is the claim — prose describing a command is commentary, and when they disagree the reader follows the command.** A fix to a procedure is not applied until the copy-pasteable part is changed, and reviewing "did I fix step 6?" by reading its sentence is precisely how this survived. Related and worth pairing: round 7's lesson was that truth-of-claims and executability are different audits; this one adds that within an executable audit, the *runnable* text outranks the text around it.

I had "handled" it with a parenthetical — *(on a public page there is no scrub step — pass the saved page itself)* — which is prose contradicting the command four lines above it, exactly the failure round 8 had just diagnosed. Fixed by giving the public variant **its own fenced command** in its own section rather than annotating the login-gated one. The class: **a single parameterised example serving two workflows is a latent contradiction, because the example can only be literally correct for one of them.** When a procedure branches, branch the *executable text*; a note saying "for the other case, substitute X" is commentary, and round 8 established that readers follow the command, not the commentary. Also worth recording that rounds 7, 8 and 9 are one defect surfacing three times — scrub-ordering, then its prose/command split, then its other branch — which is the signature of fixing an *instance* rather than the *shape*, and the shape here was "this procedure has two variants and only one is written down".

Confirmed pre-existing (not something my diff caused) by `git stash`-ing every change and re-running typecheck against the clean base, which reproduced the identical error list. `npm install` resolved it in one step. Worth recording because the failure reads exactly like a real typecheck regression at a glance — before assuming a fresh worktree's dependency tree is complete, check `node_modules` state against `package.json`, especially when the errors are all "cannot find module" for packages the manifest already lists.
- 2026-08-02 · process · closed · (#63, invoke, Task 3 — the `slotIndex`/NaN comparator regression test) a comparator that returns `NaN` on an out-of-range comparison is not reliably testable by "reproduce the buggy code path and watch it fail": `Array.prototype.sort`'s behavior on a `NaN` result is unspecified, and for small arrays V8's sort often leaves the compared pair's relative order UNCHANGED on `NaN` — so a fixture built without checking the actual row order can pass whether or not the fix is present, which would have shipped a regression test that certifies nothing. Verified by writing a throwaway Node script that ran both the buggy and the fixed comparator over several row orderings BEFORE writing the vitest test, picked the one ordering where they diverge, then confirmed the vitest test itself fails when the fix (`slotRank`'s `?? Number.POSITIVE_INFINITY` fallback) is reverted and passes when restored. Worth repeating whenever a "silently unstable sort" is the regression under test: the mechanism (NaN in a comparator) reproducing is not the same as the OUTCOME (wrong order) reproducing, and only the latter is what the test needs to pin.
- 2026-08-02 · bug · closed · (#63, invoke, test/query-events.test.ts, found before any reviewer saw it) a pre-existing test built a second `addEvent(db, {...})` call by spreading `other` — the `AddEventResult` returned by an EARLIER `addEvent` call — into the new call's input object. That was harmless while the two types happened to share every field name, but `AddEventResult` gaining `format: EventCourt[] | null` this PR (`addEvent`'s own result, distinct from `AddEventInput.format?: string`) made the spread forward a `format: null` the writer's input type does not accept, crashing at runtime (`parseEventFormat(null)`) with **no compile error** — TypeScript's excess/mismatched-property check does not fire on a spread source the way it does on a literal's own properties. Fixed by building the second call's input explicitly rather than spreading the first call's result. The general shape: a service's OWN result type and its OWN input type sharing field names is a latent coupling invisible until one of them grows a field the other cannot accept — a caller (or a test) that treats "the thing I just got back" as "the thing I pass in next" is the exact pattern that goes uncaught.
- 2026-08-02 · bug · closed · (#63, verify, Stage-4 adversarial pass, rated medium) an ORM column mode can relocate a decode OUT of the guard that was written for it. `events.format` was declared `text("format", { mode: "json" })`, so drizzle called `JSON.parse` while mapping **every row of the events table for every reader** — before `readEventFormat`, the module whose whole purpose is to fail closed on a malformed stored value, could see anything. Bytes that are not JSON therefore threw a raw `SyntaxError` out of `eventsForDay` (`tn player avail`), `match add`, and `addEvent` itself: **four commands with nothing to do with court formats**, none of which catch it, so a single hand-corrupted row broke availability recording with a stack trace. The guard was holed in precisely its own threat model — a hand-edited database is the only reason it exists. Reproduced against a real DB before any fix (`SyntaxError: Unexpected token 'o'`), then closed by making the column plain `text` and giving `event-format.ts` both `encodeEventFormat` and the `JSON.parse` inside `readEventFormat`, so exactly one module decodes it and every failure is one named `InvalidEventFormatError`. Verified migration-neutral first: both spellings emit identical DDL (`format text`), and `drizzle-kit generate` reported "No schema changes, nothing to migrate" — a column MODE is a TypeScript-side mapping decision, not a schema one, which is exactly why it is easy to choose without noticing it moved a parse

The first draft of the regression above seeded `db.insert(events).values({ format: "not json at all" })` and passed against BOTH the buggy and the fixed code — because under `{ mode: "json" }` drizzle stringifies whatever it is handed on the way IN, storing `"\"not json at all\""`, which is valid JSON. The seed path was the same path the fix changed, so the fixture could never contain the defect. Only a raw `sqlite.prepare(...).run(...)` writes the bytes a hand-edited database would have. This is the SECOND false-green in this one PR caught the same way (the first was the `NaN` comparator, logged above at `invoke`), and the shared remedy is one move: **revert the fix and watch the new test fail** before believing it. The narrower lesson is about fixture provenance — when a fix changes a write path, a fixture built with that write path is evidence about the fix, not about the defect
- 2026-08-02 · process · closed · (#63, verify) two sibling fields encoding one invariant (`slotSource: "observed" | "event-format"` beside `slotEvent: {…} | null`) produced three copies of the same non-null assertion — `slotEvent!.name`, once in each of the CLI, markdown and HTML presenters — because nothing in the type said the pair cannot disagree. Replaced with a discriminated union INTERSECTED with the rest of the record (`type LineupPlan = LineupSlotProvenance & { …rest }`), which narrows on `slotSource` and deletes all three assertions while leaving the JSON shape byte-identical, so no existing caller or test changed. Two things worth carrying: an intersection is what lets a union express a two-field invariant without turning the whole record into a union, and a test helper typed `Partial<T>` is the hole that lets the invalid state back in — the helper had to take the provenance as a WHOLE pair (a separate parameter) rather than as loose overrides, or a test could still construct a plan production code can never produce
- 2026-08-02 · process · closed · (#63, verify) `git diff main...HEAD` reviewed a diff 28 lines WIDER than the PR, because the local `main` ref was three commits behind `origin/main` — the branch was cut from `origin/main`, so the three-dot merge base resolved to the stale local ref and folded two already-merged PRs' changes into "the diff under review". It fails toward MORE review rather than less, which is why it is easy to miss: nothing looks wrong, the extra hunks are real code, and the only visible symptom here was a findings-log count that did not match (33 added entries where the run had written 6). Fixed the count and re-read the true diff against `origin/main`. The rule worth carrying: at `verify`, diff against the REMOTE base ref the PR is actually opened against (`origin/main...HEAD`), never the local branch name — a local `main` is only as fresh as the last `git pull`, and a worktree's may never have been updated at all

Fixed by resolving the format ONCE in a new Phase 0 and handing the same immutable value to every team, which also moved the refusal earlier — a bad event name now aborts with nothing written, including on a team set that would produce no lineup at all, where the per-team lookup never validated the name. Worth carrying: this module already carried three prior review findings about batch-level guarantees ("validate every leaf this batch will touch before writing ANY of them"), and the new argument reintroduced the same class one field over — **when a function already documents a whole-batch invariant, a new per-item input is a candidate violation of it by default**, and the place to look is where the item loop calls a resolver
- 2026-08-02 · process · closed · (#63, listen) reproducing a cross-PROCESS race deterministically in-process needs a trigger tied to what the FIX changed, not to a call count. The regression here fires from a `Proxy` on `db.select` — but the first attempt corrupted the row right after `select()` RETURNED, and drizzle's builder is lazy, so nothing had executed yet and the corruption landed before the very query it was meant to follow; the test then failed against the FIXED code. Moving the trigger to the start of the *second* select fixed it, because by then the first query has genuinely run. The general shape: with a lazy query builder, "after the call that reads X" and "after X has been read" are different instants, and a test that conflates them is testing an ordering that does not exist

Caught immediately here (the tests were re-run), but the safe form is to snapshot to a scratch path and copy back, which is what the other three revert-and-verify experiments in this run used. Prefer `cp` to a scratchpad over `git checkout` whenever the file has uncommitted changes worth keeping

Round 1 closed padded slots by requiring `slot.trim() === slot`; round 2 pointed out that `parseEventFormat` splits entries on `,` and takes the FIRST `:` as the separator, so a slot containing either delimiter is equally unwritable — and `{"slot":"D:1"}` was still accepted, reaching `derive.ts` as a court no supported command can record or repair. Banning `,` and `:` by name would have closed round 2 and left round 3 open; worse, a blanket "no whitespace/punctuation" rule gets it WRONG in the other direction, because `"D 1"` contains a space and IS writable (`parseEventFormat("D 1:doubles")` yields it), so the blacklist would falsely reject a legal format. Replaced both rules with one DERIVED check: run the decoded list back through `formatToText` + `parseEventFormat` and require an exact reproduction — which asks the only question that matters (*could our writer have emitted this?*) and stays correct for whatever the grammar becomes. The lesson is the one already logged for regex/delimiter parsing, arriving from a new direction: when a guard's job is "accept exactly what X produces", **execute X** rather than describing it

- 2026-08-02 · process · closed · (#63, listen round 2) a TypeScript `declare const brand: unique symbol` is type-only and does not exist at runtime, so `{ [brand]: true }` in an object literal compiles cleanly and then throws `resolvedEventFormat is not defined` on the first call. `tsc --noEmit` was green; only running the tests caught it. A branded-opaque type needs a REAL `const brand = Symbol("…")` (which TypeScript still types as `unique symbol`) when the brand is materialized in a value — `declare` is only correct for a phantom field that is never constructed. Cheap to hit, cheap to catch, and a clean reminder that a green typecheck is evidence about types, not about execution
- 2026-08-02 · bug · closed · (#63, listen round 3, Codex adversarial review, rated medium) a `unique symbol` brand does NOT make a TypeScript value opaque on its own, and the obvious hardening would have been a fix in appearance only. The brand was an ordinary object-literal property, so it was ENUMERABLE — object spread copies enumerable own *symbol* keys, and `{ ...a, slotSet: b.slotSet }` therefore produced a fully-branded value pairing event A's identity with event B's courts, cast-free and typechecking clean. The recommended remedy (non-enumerable via `Object.defineProperty`, plus a compile-time regression asserting the spread is rejected) is only HALF right, and the second half is wrong: verified against this project's own tsconfig, `{ ...a, slotSet: b.slotSet }` **still typechecks** as the branded type after the brand is made non-enumerable, because TypeScript models a spread from the DECLARED type rather than from runtime enumerability. So the compile-time regression cannot be written, and the non-enumerable brand alone changes nothing a caller would notice. What closes it is a RUNTIME check in the consumer (`getLineupPlan` refuses a value whose brand is absent), with the non-enumerable brand as the thing that makes the check discriminating. General shape: **when a guard has a compile-time half and a runtime half, verify which half is actually load-bearing before shipping the one that is easier to write** — and `readonly` is in the same family, since it is erased entirely and needed a real `Object.freeze` to stop the courts being mutated between teams

Worth recording because the escalation was VINDICATED in an unexpected direction: writing the patch surfaced that the reviewer's own recommended compile-time regression was unwritable, so the "obvious" version of this fix would have shipped looking closed while leaving the hole open. Three rounds of the same defect moving sideways is a signal about the DESIGN, and the useful output of stopping was not "do not fix it" but "the next patch needs different evidence than the last three did"

That is true for a structural object type and **too strong in general**: a NOMINAL class carrying a private field (`#opaque!: void`) makes the spread fail to typecheck, because a spread cannot carry a private field. So the accurate statement is "unwritable for a branded structural type", and the reviewer classifies the nominal-class form as optional hardening rather than a deployment-shape defect for the CLI/MCP paths. Deliberately NOT applied: the runtime assertion already closes the actual trace, and it would be the fifth patch in the sequence the fix-verification bound exists to stop — the HC made one deliberate exception to that bound, and treating that exception as standing licence is exactly the drift the rule guards against. Recorded as `accepted` (a real limitation decided against on the record) for HC triage; the general lesson is smaller and sharper than the residual: **an absolute claim about what a type system cannot express is almost always a claim about the encoding you happened to choose**, and it was worth asking the reviewer to check reasoning I was relying on rather than only to hunt for defects
- 2026-08-02 · bug · closed · (#62, PR pending) `nameKey` had TWO failure modes, not the one the issue described, and the difference decides how reachable it is. Measured against a live migrated DB rather than reasoned about: ONE OR TWO category-Cf characters left an `editDistance` of 1-2, inside `FUZZY_MAX_DISTANCE`, so tier 3 caught them and returned `ambiguous` — wrong, but LOUD, and it halts a pull to ask the HC about a player already on file; THREE OR MORE pushed the distance past the band, so the ladder fell through every tier and created a second row SILENTLY. The issue described only the silent branch, and an upstream page picks that count for free, so the branch the issue named is the reachable one rather than the unlikely one. The transferable rule is about assessment method, not Unicode: the tracked issue stated the defect CLASS correctly and its REACHABILITY not at all, and a fifteen-line probe against a real DB separated them in a way that reading the ladder could not — running the defect is cheap enough that arguing about its shape instead is the expensive option
- 2026-08-02 · process · closed · (#62) EVERY recursive `grep` in this repo silently omits `src/ingest/upsert.ts`, a 573-line core write-path file holding `upsertTeam`/`upsertPlayer` and six `nameKey` references. It contains a deliberate NUL sentinel (`const NULL_KEY = "\x00null"`, :288), so real grep classifies it `Binary file ... matches` and any `-I`/skip-binary wrapper — the harness default here — drops it from results WITH NO WARNING. Three consecutive blast-radius sweeps of this assessment missed it; it was found only by cross-checking a file READ against a grep RESULT and noticing they disagreed. Use `command grep -rn --binary-files=text` (or `-a`) for any sweep of this tree. The general shape is the dangerous part and is not specific to NUL: a deliberate control character in source changes the file's CLASSIFICATION for every content-addressed tool that walks the tree, and each of those tools reports success either way — a search returning fewer files is indistinguishable from a search finding fewer files, so the omission is invisible exactly when a sweep is being trusted to be exhaustive. Test files here now write format characters as `\u` escapes with named consts rather than literals, so the tests for this defect cannot set the same trap
- 2026-08-02 · process · closed · (#62) `ship`'s delegated `assess` exploration sub-agent NEVER RETURNED — three messages (a status nudge, a correction, an explicit final call) all went unanswered, and the phase completed only because the orchestrator ran the exploration inline under ADR 0003's graceful degradation. Two things worth keeping. First, the degradation clause is what made this a non-event rather than a stall, but nothing TRIGGERED it: there is no timeout, no liveness signal, and no way to distinguish 'still working' from 'dead' — the orchestrator has to decide to give up, and an orchestrator that trusts its own delegation policy will wait indefinitely. Second, and more useful: `ship`'s own delegation criterion (`delegate by OUTPUT-WEIGHT, not per phase`) already answered whether to re-delegate the `invoke` loop — a one-line fold change with a dense Unicode test matrix is signal-heavy and output-light, the exact inverse of the offload test — so running it inline was the policy applied correctly rather than a second degradation. The rule the phase table obscures: the table's rows are a DEFAULT keyed on the typical shape of each phase, and the output-weight criterion above it is the actual test

It replaced the leaf via `unlinkSync` + immediate `writeFileSync` at the same path, and ext4 routinely REUSES the freed inode number for the next file created in that directory, so `unlinkIfStillOurs` compared `{dev, ino}`, found a match, and deleted the replacement — the precise data loss the guard exists to prevent, in the test written to prove it does not happen. APFS does not recycle that way, so the machine I developed on could not see it. Fixed by `linkSync`-ing the original inode under a second name before unlinking, which makes reuse impossible on any filesystem (an inode with a surviving link is never reallocated) and makes the test assert the guard's behavior rather than the allocator's. Two lessons: a test that constructs "a different file" by deleting and recreating at the same path is testing the inode allocator, not identity; and NOTHING local caught this — five adversarial rounds, three mutation sweeps, and a reviewer that read the test all passed, because every one of them ran on the same filesystem
- 2026-08-02 · fact · closed · (#65, PR #83) the inode-reuse behavior above is a real limit on `unlinkIfStillOurs` itself, not only on the test: `{dev, ino}` does not survive an unlink-and-recreate, so an actor who REPLACES a leaf at the same path can produce a file the guard reads as "still ours" and deletes. The existing `nlink` sample does not help — it catches a second name for OUR inode, not a different file wearing its number after we released it. Not closable by comparing more `Stats` fields (a recreate can coincide on those too, and a usually-right heuristic is the shape this module keeps retracting); it needs `unlinkat` against a directory handle, which pure Node does not expose — the same wall as #74's accepted TOCTOU set. So the honest scope is "best-effort against the swap it was built for (a redirected directory component, where the target genuinely is a different inode), not a general proof of ownership", and that is now what the code says
- 2026-08-02 · bug · closed · (#65, PR #83, Codex round 7, rated critical) the cross-leaf rollback deleted by pathname after a `{dev, ino}` ownership check, and that identity is RECYCLABLE — so a replacement file that inherited our freed inode number could be deleted, converting a non-destructive partial failure (an orphan) into destructive cleanup. The fix is ORDERING, not a better identity check: unlink while the descriptor is STILL OPEN, then close. An inode with a live descriptor can never be freed, so it can never be reallocated, so nothing else can be wearing its number at the moment of the check — a real closure that does not depend on timing or on the filesystem's allocation policy. Three things worth keeping. The reviewer was right that `unlinkat` would NOT close this (it anchors the directory lookup, not the inode) and I had written exactly that wrong claim into the residual; the reviewer then concluded no pure-Node closure existed and recommended withdrawing the rollback, which was also wrong; and the correct answer was in the same PR's own TEST, which had already used `linkSync` to pin an inode for precisely this reason — the production fix was sitting in the test fixture for a full round before anyone read it as a design
- 2026-08-02 · process · closed · (#65, PR #83) `fsync` before `close` is what makes fd-anchored cleanup possible at all, and the reasoning generalizes past this module: deferred writeback errors (ENOSPC/EIO on NFS, FUSE, some local filesystems) surface at CLOSE, and cleanup after a failed close is unsound because the descriptor is already gone and the inode may already be recycled. Forcing those errors out at `fsync`, while the descriptor is still held, converts an unrecoverable failure into a recoverable one. It also splits the two cases honestly: an fsync failure means the bytes are not durable, so delete; a close failure AFTER a successful fsync means the bytes ARE durable and only the descriptor is in doubt, so keep the file rather than delete by a path whose ownership can no longer be proven. The two outcomes are deliberately opposite, and each now has its own test so a later reader does not "fix" the asymmetry
- 2026-08-02 · process · closed · (#65, PR #83) the regression for the recycled-inode fix is VACUOUS on the development machine: macOS/APFS does not recycle that way, so reverting the ordering to close-then-unlink leaves the whole suite green locally. Its authority comes entirely from Linux CI, where that exact construction had already been observed deleting the replacement. So the mutation sweep — which had killed every other fix in this PR — returned a false all-clear here, and the honest record is "verified by CI on ext4", not "verified by mutation". Two rules fall out: a test whose subject is kernel/filesystem BEHAVIOR must name the platform it is meaningful on, in the test, because a later reader on a green machine will otherwise assume it is load-bearing everywhere; and do NOT make such a test deterministic by mocking the OS primitive it depends on — faking the allocator here would defeat the real fix too, and the test would then pass against a broken implementation

It created the replacement file while the set writer still HELD the first leaf's descriptor, so the inode could not be recycled and the identity check skipped the replacement under BOTH orderings; reverting the fix left it green everywhere. I had labelled it "vacuous on macOS, authoritative on Linux CI", which read as a careful limitation and was in fact wrong in a way that made the critical guarantee untested. The tell I missed: I wrote the platform caveat from the ORIGINAL false-green's diagnosis (APFS does not recycle) and never re-derived it for the REWRITTEN test, whose construction had changed underneath the caveat. A stated limitation is a claim like any other and goes stale when the code it describes is edited — an honest-sounding caveat is not evidence of having checked
- 2026-08-02 · process · closed · (#65, PR #83) the fix for the above is the general lesson: when a guarantee rests on an environment-dependent behavior (inode recycling, locale, filesystem semantics, clock), assert the INVARIANT the code controls rather than the OUTCOME the environment produces. The behavioral test ("the replacement survives") can only fail where the kernel recycles; the ordering test ("unlink was called before close", asserted as the exact call sequence) fails everywhere, immediately, with a legible diff — and it is the one that actually guards the critical fix. Keep both: the behavioral one states the threat, the invariant one is the regression net. This is the same move as deriving a guard from structure rather than enumerating cases, applied to test design instead of to code

One genuine red became five here — a mocked `runMigrations` kept throwing through four unrelated `db migrate` cases — and the four false reds are louder than the one true one, so the first instinct is to debug the cascade rather than read the signal. The shape to watch: a fix whose mechanism is a coercion or a parse is itself partial, and the question "what input makes MY replacement throw?" has to be asked of the new line, not only of the old one

But `$("input, meta")` returns only *elements*, and HTML has containers whose contents the parser deliberately produces as **text**: `<noscript>` is RAWTEXT while scripting is enabled (cheerio's default), `<textarea>`/`<title>` are RCDATA in every mode, plus `<iframe>`, `<noframes>`, `<noembed>`, `<xmp>`. Two things generalise. **Parsing correctly and reading the parse correctly are different skills**, and switching to a parser retires the lexical defect class while quietly introducing a *parser-context* class that no amount of care about regexes would have surfaced. And the reviewer's recommended fix — re-parse with `scriptingEnabled: false` — would have closed `<noscript>` **only**: correct for the instance it reported, class **C1** if taken literally. The hit rate was 100%: not one call produced the six characters that were typed. The tell each time was that the file went binary and `grep` stopped seeing it — so the **symptom of the very bug being fixed** was the only reason any of them surfaced. This generalises past NUL to every `\uXXXX`, `\n` or `\t` an author means literally, and it is invisible in an editor and in every diff view. Three things that work, in order of reliability: build control-character test data from **byte arrays** (`Buffer.from([0x00])`), which has no escape to mangle; where the escape *text* is genuinely required, write a placeholder token and substitute it with a shell command whose quoting you control end to end; and — not optional, since the first two only reduce the odds — **re-read the file's bytes after writing**, never trusting that what was typed is what landed

The disagreement is now gone from this repo (no tracked file holds a NUL, enforced by `test/source-no-nul-bytes.test.ts`) but the property of the tools is unchanged, and the next repo has it

The behavioral test still earned its place, as the **measurement** rather than the regression: written before the edit, run at both commits, green at both, it is what turns "byte-identical" from a claim into evidence. Naming that in the plan mattered more than the test did — an unlabelled always-green test reads to a reviewer as a test that cannot fail, which is a real defect class, and the label is the only thing distinguishing it from one. Stage a NUL, revert the working-tree copy without staging the revert, and the guard reports clean while `git commit` writes the staged blob that still carries the byte — reproduced before fixing: 10/10 green with a NUL at offset 10 of the staged `docs/findings.md`. The generalisable shape is **snapshot consistency**: the moment a check derives its *subject list* from one source and its *evidence* from another, it can only certify the intersection, and it will not say so. Note what makes this class nastier than an ordinary bug — the guard was written specifically to stop an invisible failure, and its own failure was invisible in the same way, so a green run was evidence of nothing. Also note the fix the reviewer proposed (read index blobs instead) would have closed the reported trace and **opened its mirror** — a NUL written but not staged, invisible to an index-only scan — which is class C1 again; both snapshots are now scanned and every offender names the one it came from
- 2026-08-03 · bug · closed · (#66, PR #89, Codex adversarial review round 1 — class A, rated Low) **the failure message asserted something the same file's own doc comment disproved four lines up.** `describeOffenders` said *"Every content-search tool then reports ZERO matches in this file"*, while the header table in the same file recorded `git grep` finding 3 hits and `grep -ac` finding 3. Both cannot be true. What makes this worth a line rather than a typo fix: a **diagnostic** is read at the worst possible moment, by someone who has just been told their file is broken and who has no independent way to check the explanation — so an overstatement there propagates further than the same sentence in a comment would. The claim-vs-code lens has to be pointed at error strings, not only at asserting code, and I had declared that exact lens and still walked past it — the text was written to persuade rather than to describe, which is the tell
- 2026-08-03 · bug · closed · (#66, PR #89, Codex FIX-VERIFICATION pass — two class-A defects **in the fixes for the previous round's findings**, which is the case PROJECT.md's escalate-on-recurrence bound exists for) **a byte-level fix written in string-level code reintroduces the encoding assumption it was closing.** Round 1's fix made the guard read index blobs so list and bytes shared a snapshot — correct — but the working-tree pass still decoded `git ls-files -z` with `encoding: "utf8"`. A git pathname is a **byte** string: `0x80` decodes to U+FFFD, re-encodes to `EF BF BD`, and `existsSync` then reports a present file absent, so the pass skipped it and a NUL written-but-not-staged in that file was seen by neither pass. The second defect was an **off-by-one at the end of a stream**: `start + size > buf.length` accepts a response whose final byte is the last content byte, so `"abc blob 1\nX"` — body present, git's trailing LF missing — framed cleanly and returned an object, while the test above it claimed truncated responses fail closed. Both generalise the same way: **the fix inherited the frame of the thing it was fixing.** A snapshot-consistency fix was written in the string domain that caused the original class, and a length-framing fix checked the length without checking the delimiter that proves the length was honoured. Worth recording separately: the non-UTF-8 trace is **unreachable on macOS** — APFS refuses such names with `errno 92 EILSEQ`, verified from shell, Python and Node — and fully reachable on Linux, where CI runs. A local-only reproduction attempt would have "passed" and been read as refutation

The rewrite dispatched on the link **TARGET**'s shape (`*/*`) while splitting the **SELF** path, so the two disagreed exactly when one had a slash and the other did not: a slashless target (`ln -s tn launcher`) took the wildcard arm and resolved `./tn` **against the caller's working directory** — the original #87 defect, restored, inside the commit whose message says it closes the class. The tell is a `case` whose subject and whose operand are different variables; that mismatch is invisible when every fixture happens to make both shapes agree, and all six existing cases did. Generalises the C3 class with a sharper instrument than "re-read the fix": **name the variable each branch dispatches on and the variable it mutates, and if they differ, construct the input where their shapes disagree**

A planned bound was withdrawn on this measurement rather than shipped as untestable defensive code
- 2026-08-03 · process · closed · (#87, PR #88) **`execvp` resolves a bare command name to a full path before the kernel sees it, so `$0` never arrives slashless from a PATH invocation** — a test written to pin "the launcher handles a `$0` with no slash" therefore passed against the defect and proved nothing. Rejected as a false green and rewritten to run the interpreter directly (`sh tn --help`), which is the only form that puts a bare name in `$0`. Sibling of the macOS `/var`->`/private/var` case in the same run: **both were tests that failed or passed for a platform reason rather than the reason claimed, and only running them against the known-broken code exposed it**
- 2026-08-03 · process · closed · (#87, PR #88) **removing a dependency closes a class that guarding it only narrows.** A review found one unchecked helper (`readlink`); the fix guarded it; the next pass found the same class open through two unchecked `dirname` calls. The durable answer was to stop calling `dirname` at all — POSIX parameter expansion has no failure mode to check — leaving exactly one external command, the one that is guarded. Reach for *can this call be deleted?* before *should this call be checked?*, because the second question has to be asked again for every call site and the first is asked once

Promoted to the register (#76, #77) with the distinction stated in the BODY rather than in a new label, because inventing a fourth state mid-pass would itself have been the fix-the-instance move. - 2026-08-03 · process · closed · (#90, PR #91, two consecutive fix-verification passes on the AC's own fixes) **an invariant that crosses a public boundary cannot be enforced by a structural type, and two attempts to prove otherwise both shipped a hole.** A report API took `since` and a season `label` as separate arguments, so a caller could filter to 2025 and print 2026. Attempt 1 branded the pair with a real unexported `Symbol` — copied verbatim by `Object.assign`, because a symbol key is *enumerable*, so a mismatched clone typechecked with no cast. Attempt 2 reduced the type to a single `year` field so there was "nothing to disagree with" — which killed the clone but left an **accessor**: `{ get year() { return ++n <= 2 ? "2025" : "2026" } }` answers differently for the boundary read and the label read, and the consumer read it three times. What finally closed it is the thing the reviewer recommended before attempt 1: **read the value once into a local, validate it, derive everything from that primitive.** The generalisable form: *a type describes what a caller declares; only a read describes what a caller did.* When a value arrives from outside the module, count the reads before reaching for the type system — and note that `Object.freeze` protects the object you froze, never a clone of it

Where a guard's input becomes a *string comparand* rather than a parsed value, the malformed case is not a crash — it is a silently different query, so validate the shape (four ASCII digits) rather than trusting the derivation

Measured contrast on the same DB: a corrupt `format` printed `report build status=error message="stored event format is not valid JSON — …"`, a corrupt `league_scope` printed nothing. Two lessons. The concurrency half of "one read, two columns" was written down in this same commit while the refusal half went unexamined, so **noticing that a column is new in one dimension does not carry to the others**. Normalization is closable and was closed: `"Café"` composed vs `"Cafe"+U+0301` decomposed, and full-width `"Ｍｉｘｅｄ"`, are the same league to a reader and different strings to `startsWith`, so the fold is now `normalize("NFKC").toLowerCase()` — the same NFKC `src/db/name-key.ts` applies to names, minus its punctuation stripping, since a league context's `+`/`.`/`-` are load-bearing (`Adult 40+ 3.5`). Full case folding is NOT closable in plain JS — `ß` lowercases to `ß`, never `ss`, and there is no case-folding table exposed — so `exclude:STRASSE` does not match `Straße 3.5`. That limitation is now **stated in the doc comment and pinned by a test asserting the documented behavior**, rather than left implied by a comment that called the fold "Unicode-aware". Re-prompting it recovered the full report with its context intact, so recovery is cheap, but only if the caller notices it needs recovering. Treat "idle with no delivered artifact" as a **distinct outcome** from "still running", the same way `PROJECT.md` → *Reviewer* deliberately keeps `unreachable` and `timed-out` distinct rather than collapsing them; the generalisation is that a delegation's completion signal and its deliverable are two separate facts, and only the second one is evidence

The drift guard that would catch an omission (`test/parity_check_test.rb`) is never vendored, and `.github/workflows/parity.yml` gates that step on `hashFiles(...) != ''`, so it silently skips here. The trap it sets is quiet: a new doc that assumes domain terms are established "in the glossary" is assuming a file that does not exist, and the assumption reads as reasonable because a file by that name is right there

What matters is the disposition: the file's own convention is *annotate, don't silently diverge*, and the new document states the divergence and links the spec as history rather than editing a record the HC has been correcting by hand

The general shape for guarding any artifact with a test: the cases that read the artifact give you the red, and the cases that feed the same predicate synthetic input are what stop the green from being vacuous — an artifact guard with only the first kind passes the moment its extraction breaks

A markdown link target escaped the scan entirely. The sibling finding ran the other way: `FENCED_BLOCK` recognised only paired triple-backticks, so a tilde fence, a longer fence, or an unclosed one leaked its illustration into the scan and would have **reddened** over a path that was never a claim. Both are the same root — the guard enumerated one spelling of a construct instead of deriving from the construct's actual grammar — but they fail in opposite directions, and only the first is invisible. The fix tokenizes span contents, scans link targets, and closes fences by Markdown's real rule (3+ of either character, longer-or-equal closes, EOF terminates)

Codex rated the same hole class A. The tell is the tense: a limitation is a property of the design, but "a future edit would be unguarded" is a **conditional defect**, and the condition is one someone else's PR satisfies without knowing the condition exists. When a self-review sentence names an input that would defeat the check, the sentence is a finding, not a caveat — and writing it down in the artifact the reviewer reads does not convert it into one that has been dispositioned

But `ARCHITECTURE.md` indents list continuations four spaces, so stripping indentation would have silently removed real prose from the scan — trading a *visible* false red (an illustrative path reddens the suite, someone fixes it in a minute) for a *silent* false green (a real dead path in a continuation line never checked again). Measured before deciding: 4 such lines already exist in the document. The rule that generalises is not "handle every Markdown construct" but **when two failure directions are available, take the one a human will see** — and record the asymmetry where the omission is, so the next reader does not "complete" it

The prose was right, the regex did the opposite, and the test written to pin the policy used an indented *list continuation* rather than an indented *fence*, so it passed over the hole. Two lessons, and the second is the transferable one: a policy stated in a comment beside code is not enforcement, and **the test that pins a policy must use the construct the policy is about** — mine pinned the easy half. CommonMark's actual bound is three spaces, which is also what makes `^ {0,3}` the right spelling
- 2026-08-04 · process · closed · (#101, PR #103) **the correction had already been made once in this repo, on the same class, and I did not reach for it.** `test/cli-grammar-parity.test.ts:13-21` carries `^ {0,3}` with a comment naming it as Reviewer finding 1 on PR #84 and explaining the CommonMark leading-indent rule — the identical fix to the identical mistake, sitting in the sibling test file I had read that same session while matching house style. I copied the file's *voice* (heavy justifying comments, a pure extractor, vacuity guards) and missed its *content*. Worth stating as a search habit rather than a resolution: when writing a new scanner over a text format, grep the existing tests for the format's own edge-case corrections first — a repo that has been reviewed adversarially for months has already paid for those, and they are recorded exactly where a new author is least likely to look, in a comment on a regex in a neighbouring file
- 2026-08-04 · process · closed · (#101, PR #103, two fix-verification passes) **bounding a scanner by POLICY terminates; chasing the format's grammar does not.** Round 2 returned five A findings against the round-1 fixes — block-quoted fences, closing fences with trailing text, balanced parentheses in a link destination, percent-encoding, reference-style definitions, punctuation-wrapped tokens. Trying to satisfy CommonMark would have been round 3, 4, 5 (the shape the existing regex-HTML-parsing entry already records), and no Markdown parser is in this repo's dependencies. What terminated it was sorting the residue by FAILURE DIRECTION rather than by construct: every input that failed *silent* was closed (3 of them), and every input that fails *loud* was left, bounded by a written statement that the scanner is a heuristic over a document whose conventions it also constrains. The generalisable move is that "which constructs does this handle?" has no natural stopping point, while "which constructs can fail without anyone noticing?" is a finite list you can actually finish

Recorded because the instruction to "STOP and report" a wrong plan fact is calibrated to facts that change the work; this one didn't, and separating "wrong but harmless to the fix" from "wrong and load-bearing" is itself the judgment call worth naming.
