Low-stakes here since the file's real defect (the `:19-20` "repo-root-relative" claim needing a cwd-relative-override caveat) was named correctly and fixed — but a citation sweep is worth spot-checking against the actual file before trusting the line numbers, the same way code line numbers drift after upstream edits.
- 2026-08-05 · bug · closed · (#111, PR #116, Codex round 1, class A/medium) **`existsSync` cannot classify why an open failed, and using it as an error classifier made a fail-open.** The missing-database guard asked `!existsSync(path)` after a failed `new Database(..., { fileMustExist: true })` and called any `false` "the database is missing". Measured: a path whose parent is a **regular file** (ENOTDIR) and one whose parent is **not traversable** (EACCES) both report `existsSync === false` exactly like a genuinely absent file, and better-sqlite3 flattens all three to the same `SQLITE_CANTOPEN`. So a misconfigured `TN_DB_PATH` told the operator to run `tn db migrate` — which cannot repair ENOTDIR — *and* `writeRequestLogRow`'s `MissingDatabaseError` carve-out then silently discarded the one signal pointing at the real storage failure. The self-review had tested the sad path in only one direction (a directory AT the leaf, which *does* exist, so it rethrew correctly) and never the direction where the path is unopenable but absent-looking. **A boolean cannot answer a "why" question**; only the raw `open(2)` errno separates them. The tell: a comment asserting that a `false` from a two-state predicate uniquely implies one of three causes
- 2026-08-05 · process · closed · (#111, PR #116, Codex round 1) **the reviewer's finding was right and its recommended fix was wrong, and running the recommendation is what showed the difference.** Codex found the misclassification above and recommended "classify only a positively identified SQLite missing-file error (for example, its errno/code)". A three-case probe took two minutes and refuted it: better-sqlite3 returns `SQLITE_CANTOPEN` for absent-file, ENOTDIR and EACCES alike, so there is no SQLite code to classify by and the recommended fix would have shipped the same defect wearing a more authoritative comment. The working discriminator was one layer down — Node's own `open(2)` errno. This is the second recorded instance of a correct diagnosis arriving with an unusable remedy, and the cost of checking is far below the cost of implementing a plausible one: **treat a reviewer's finding and its recommendation as two separate claims, and verify the second the way you verified the first**
- 2026-08-05 · bug · closed · (#111, PR #116, Codex fix-verification pass 1, class A/medium) **a dangling symlink reads as ENOENT, so the errno fix for the previous finding was itself wrong — and the third predicate would have been wrong too.** `TN_DB_PATH` pointing at a symlink whose target's parent is absent makes `open(2)` return ENOENT exactly like a never-bootstrapped database, so the guard promised `tn db migrate` would fix it; it cannot (migrate `mkdirSync`s the LINK's directory, which already exists, and SQLite still fails to create the target). Measured all seven states against three predicates: `existsSync` misses four, `open(2)` errno misses the two dangling-symlink rows, and `lstat` fixes the leaf-symlink row but **breaks a legitimate symlink to a database that has simply not been created yet**, while still missing the ancestor row. **No single-call predicate matches the column it was being asked to compute.** The error was in the question: the predicate was being asked to prove that a FUTURE command would succeed. Fixed by making the message report the observed errno and offer the remedy conditionally ("if it has not been created yet"), which is true on every row including the ones the predicate cannot separate. **When two successive boundary revisions are each locally justified and neither converges, stop sharpening the predicate and check what claim it is being asked to support** — a predicate that only reports an observation cannot leak the way one that promises an outcome does
- 2026-08-05 · bug · closed · (#111, PR #116 — closes the `bug · open · (#110, verify — belongs to #111)` entry above) **`tn db backup` against a missing database no longer leaves an empty database behind.** That entry routed the defect here deliberately: the service layer never created the file, `dispatch`'s telemetry wrapper did, after the command had already refused. Both halves of #111's fix close it — `openDb` no longer `mkdirSync`s or creates by default, and `writeRequestLogRow` returns silently on `MissingDatabaseError` instead of manufacturing a database to fail to insert into. Pinned by `test/cli-db-backup-command.test.ts` case 17, verified red against the pre-fix `openDb`. Worth noting how close this came to staying invisible: the pre-existing case 15 *appeared* to cover it (`expect(existsSync(missingPath)).toBe(false)`) while its own comment admitted it had to block the parent with a regular file so telemetry would fail too — **an assertion that was true only because the fixture arranged a second failure to mask the first.** A test whose comment explains why it had to avoid the real case is a defect report, not coverage
- 2026-08-05 · process · closed · (#111, PR #116, final) **a PR that merged mid-run falsified a comment and a test's rationale in code my branch had never touched.** #110 landed while #111 was in review, and its `src/db/backup.ts` carried *"never `openDb()` — `openDb()` `mkdirSync`s the parent and creates an empty database"* as the stated reason for opening SQLite directly. #111 made that reason false. The textual merge conflict was one file (`docs/findings.md`, both sides appending); the *semantic* conflicts were in three files git merged cleanly and silently — a source comment, a test's justifying comment, and an open findings entry addressed to this very issue. **`git merge` reports overlapping lines, never overlapping claims.** After integrating a base branch mid-run, re-read what the incoming code SAYS about the code you changed, not just whether the lines conflict — the entry addressed to #111 was found only by reading the conflict hunk it happened to sit inside
- 2026-08-05 · bug · closed · (#111, PR #116, Codex attestation review, class A/medium) **a fourth semantic conflict the merge sweep missed, and the sweep's own commit message claimed there were exactly three.** `test/db-backup.test.ts` scenario 3's comment called its two assertions "the regression guard against ever routing this check back through `openDb()`, which `mkdirSync`s the parent" — false after #111, and worse, **the assertions would now stay green through exactly the refactor they were written to catch**, because the behavior they detect no longer distinguishes the two paths. A stale rationale is not merely a wrong comment: it silently converts a real guard into a test that passes either way while every reader still counts it as coverage. Replaced with scenario 3b, which pins the reason `backup.ts` NOW gives for opening directly — a backup source must not have `openDb`'s `journal_mode = WAL` applied — measured first (a DELETE-mode source stays `delete` through a direct open, becomes `wal` permanently once the pragma runs) and verified red by adding that pragma. **When a change falsifies a comment that justifies a guard, re-derive whether the guard still discriminates; the comment and the assertion rot at different rates and only the comment is visible in review**
- 2026-08-05 · process · closed · (#111, PR #116, Codex attestation rounds) **renaming a helper mid-review left three test comments naming a symbol that no longer existed, and four successive review sweeps each found exactly one more rotted claim.** `isAbsentPath` became `openFailureCode` when the predicate was reframed; the rename compiled cleanly because the stale references were all in COMMENTS, which no typechecker reads. The reviewer named two sites; a `grep` for the dead symbol found three. That ratio is the lesson twice over — **after renaming anything, grep for the old name across comments and docs, not just code, because the compiler cannot see the half that matters here** — and separately: a reviewer's enumeration of instances is a sample, not a census, so re-derive the full set mechanically before calling a class closed. The wider pattern across this PR's four attestation sweeps (a stale rationale in `db-backup`, a stale describe label in `fs-output-root`, a dead symbol in three test comments) is that **prose rots silently while its tests keep passing**, and every one of them was invisible to a green suite
- 2026-08-06 · process · closed · (#117, PR pending, assess) **the issue named one mechanism and the symptom had two; fixing the named one would have removed half the failures and left the bug reported.** #117 was filed from a Codex review of PR #116 describing a stale pre-transaction journal read in drizzle's migrator. Reproducing it before designing the fix — 11 of 20 rounds at two concurrent processes — showed **two** independent causes behind the one symptom: that read (6 of 11) and `PRAGMA journal_mode = WAL` not honouring SQLite's busy handler while two processes convert the same fresh database (5 of 11), which throws in `openDb` *before* the migrator is reached and which the obvious `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` fix therefore cannot touch. The stack trace is what separated them; the error strings alone read as one bug. **A reported mechanism is a hypothesis about the cause, not a census of it — reproduce first and attribute every observed failure to a cause before designing against the one you were handed**
- 2026-08-06 · process · closed · (#117, assess) **the issue's own "not empirically reproduced" caveat was the highest-value thing in it, and clearing it cost eight minutes.** The filing was honest that the reviewer's trace had never been run. Running it produced the second mechanism above, the blast-radius bound that kept the fix off ~30 unrelated call sites, and a measured fix design — none of which was reachable by reading. **When an issue states that its central claim is unverified, verifying it is the first task of the fix, not a nicety**; the cost is bounded and the finding rate was three for one here
- 2026-08-06 · fact · closed · (#117, assess) **more concurrency measured *less* of this race, not more.** Two concurrent `tn db migrate` processes failed in ~47% of rounds; four failed in 2 of 15. Extra processes add startup jitter that spreads them past each other rather than into each other. The regression test is sized at two processes for that reason. **For a startup-window race, fan-out is not a proxy for pressure — measure the rate at each width instead of assuming it rises**
- 2026-08-06 · bug · closed · (#117, invoke, self-review) **a red-first test came back green for the wrong reason, and only running it red exposed that.** The ordering assertion `expect(begin).toBeLessThan(bookkeeping)` passed against the unfixed code because `findIndex` returns `-1` when there is no `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` at all, and `-1` is less than every index — so the one case that should have failed hardest reported success. Fixed by asserting `begin >= 0` first. **An index-comparison assertion has a sentinel that satisfies it vacuously; running the test red is what distinguishes "the property holds" from "the property could not be evaluated"** — the same shape as `rules/testing.md`'s fixture/exit-condition rule, one layer down
- 2026-08-06 · process · closed · (#117, invoke) **an ordering defect is invisible to every end-state test, which is why this one survived a suite that already covered it.** `test/db-migrate.test.ts` asserted the migrated schema and idempotency, and both were true before and after the fix — a finished database cannot show the order its statements arrived in. The deterministic guard came from an observation seam that already existed for an unrelated reason (`openDb`'s `verbose`, added in #32 to count queries), forwarded one level. **Before accepting that a concurrency defect can only be tested by racing it, check whether the invariant can be stated as a statement ORDER and whether the codebase already has a seam that shows one**
- 2026-08-06 · bug · closed · (#117, invoke, mutation sweep) **a test comment claimed to kill a branch it could not, and the sweep caught the claim rather than the branch.** The WAL retry's `code !== "SQLITE_BUSY"` fast-fail was covered by a case asserting `enableWal` on a readonly connection throws a readonly error — with the comment *"without this case … a mutation removing it would survive."* Removing the guard, the readonly error is simply retried to exhaustion and then thrown, so the assertion passed unchanged and the mutant lived. The distinguishing observable was never the error type but the **attempt count**, which the test was not measuring. Fixed by counting `journal_mode = WAL` statements through the `verbose` seam (1 for fail-fast, 3 for exhaust). **A fail-fast guard is invisible to any assertion about WHAT is thrown, because the slow path throws the same thing — assert how much work happened before the throw**; and a test comment naming the mutation it kills is a claim to execute, not a note to trust (class C2, and the sweep is what executed it)
- 2026-08-06 · bug · closed · (#117, PR #120, verify — guard-completeness lens) **the busy-retry guard enumerated one member of a SQLite error FAMILY, and only a stub could have caught it.** `enableWal` retried on `code !== "SQLITE_BUSY"`, but SQLite defines SQLITE_BUSY as a family and better-sqlite3 reports the **extended** name — measured: a write on a stale WAL read snapshot raises `SQLITE_BUSY_SNAPSHOT`. A `SQLITE_BUSY_RECOVERY` during conversion would therefore have been classified "not contention" and failed fast, reinstating the exact symptom the retry exists to remove, on the rarer path. What makes this worth recording is the **testability shape**: every state a real database can reach on this path raises the BARE code, so a real-database test can only ever exercise the member already covered — the enumeration is invisible to any fixture built the obvious way. A stub raising the extended code kills it. **When a guard matches an error code, check whether the platform defines that code as a family; and if the reachable states only produce one member, that is a reason to stub, not a reason to conclude the guard is complete**
- 2026-08-06 · bug · closed · (#117, PR #120, verify — claim-vs-code lens) **a comment bounding a new code path was falsified by fixtures inside the same repo.** `enableWal`'s doc claimed the WAL-conversion retry is "reached only on the first open of a brand-new file" and that the other ~30 `openDb` call sites "never enter it". Both false: any database not yet in WAL enters the loop, including the delete-mode fixtures the legacy-upgrade tests build with a bare `new Database(path)`. The claim conflated *entering the loop* (normal, harmless) with *contending* (needs two converters at once) — only the second is bounded. Checked one counterexample that did NOT hold, too: a `.backup()` destination inherits `wal`, so a restored backup is not one. **A bound written about a new code path should be checked against the repo's own test fixtures before the outside world — they are the cheapest counterexamples available and they are already on disk**
- 2026-08-06 · bug · closed · (#117, PR #120, Codex round 1, class B) **using `exec` instead of drizzle's `prepare().run()` in a reimplemented migrator turned a loud failure silent, and the equivalence test could not see it.** `applyMigrations` ran each `--> statement-breakpoint` chunk with `sqlite.exec()`; drizzle uses `prepare().run()`, which REJECTS a multi-statement string ("The supplied SQL string contains more than one statement"). Since a chunk is meant to be exactly one statement, that rejection is a guard — and `exec` discarded it, so a hand-authored migration missing a breakpoint would run both halves and record itself applied. Executed the reviewer's trace: `exec` ran `CREATE TABLE audit …; DROP TABLE teams;` and the table was gone. This repo hand-authors migration DML (0001, 0010), so it is a live shape. **The byte-identical-output equivalence test was blind to it by construction — two implementations agree on every input the current fixtures contain, and the difference lives entirely in inputs they do not.** When reimplementing a dependency's loop, diff the PRIMITIVES, not only the outputs; a more permissive primitive is a removed guard wearing the same result
- 2026-08-06 · bug · closed · (#117, PR #120, Codex round 1, class B) **"a guard no test can kill" was my justification for omitting one, and it was false — the reviewer killed it in one trace.** `applyMigrations` rolled back unconditionally in its catch, with a comment arguing SQLite's automatic-rollback classes were unreachable because a constraint failure aborts the statement rather than the transaction. True, and irrelevant: cap a database with `PRAGMA max_page_count` and a migration statement raises `SQLITE_FULL` with `inTransaction` already false, after which the bare `ROLLBACK` throws "cannot rollback - no transaction is active" and REPLACES "database or disk is full" — the operator debugs the wrong error. The reasoning was sound about the failure the change had actually produced and was presented as a property of the code. **`rules/testing.md`'s "never keep a branch no test can kill" is about branches you have PROVEN unkillable; using it to justify omitting a guard inverts it into a licence to skip the proof** — and the proof here was one pragma
- 2026-08-06 · bug · closed · (#117, PR #120, listen) **the first version of the regression test for the finding above passed against the code it was written to reject, and the reason was a second-order property of SQLite.** It capped the database so the FIRST migration statement failed — but a single-statement failure leaves `inTransaction === true`, because SQLite can still undo one statement; the bare `ROLLBACK` then succeeds and nothing distinguishes guarded from unguarded. Measured seven configurations: single-statement failures never abandoned the transaction, and a failure after earlier statements in the same transaction had dirtied and spilled pages did so 7 times out of 7. The working test therefore has to be many small writes, which is `applyMigrations`'s real shape anyway. **Reproducing the error CODE is not reproducing the STATE — when a guard keys on state (`inTransaction`), the fixture has to be built against that state, and running the mutant is the only thing that tells them apart**
- 2026-08-06 · process · closed · (#117, PR #120, Codex round 1) **the reviewer's most useful output was not a defect but a boundary on what two of my tests proved.** It observed that the already-WAL case passes against the pre-fix one-liner (it pins the premise that bounds the fix, not the retry), that both equivalence tests would pass if `applyMigrations` were replaced wholesale by drizzle again (they are compatibility tests, not serialization guards), and that the end-to-end concurrent test asserted table names and journal count but not indexes — so an interleaving that skipped an index while recording its journal row would pass. None is a defect; together they re-describe what the suite actually guarantees. **Ask a reviewer what each test proves, not only whether the code is right — a test that pins a premise is worth keeping and worth not mistaking for a guard**, and the third observation was cheap to close by comparing the full `sqlite_master` against an uncontended migration
- 2026-08-06 · bug · closed · (#117, PR #120, Codex fix-verification pass 1, class B) **a regression test's budget was denominated in pages, so a different SQLite page size turned it into a no-op.** The `SQLITE_FULL` test capped the database at `page_count + 20` — 80 KiB at the 4 KiB default, but 640 KiB at a 32 KiB page size, where all 2000 inserts fit and no failure occurs at all. Replayed across six page sizes: 512 B stops after 40 statements, 4 KiB → 380, 8 KiB → 764, 16 KiB → 1542, **32 KiB and 64 KiB → never**. On those builds the case fails at its own "did not fail at all" assertion rather than exercising the guard, so it is loud rather than silent — but it tests nothing. Fixed by pinning `page_size = 4096` before WAL and before any content (the order is load-bearing; WAL needs a VACUUM to change it afterwards) **and asserting the pin took**, so a future SQLite ignoring it reddens instead of reverting to the same hole. **A test budget expressed in a unit the platform is free to redefine is a portability bug wearing a constant** — denominate in bytes, or pin the unit and assert the pin
- 2026-08-06 · bug · closed · (#117, PR #120, Codex fix-verification pass 1, class C) **a findings entry stated its own lesson backwards, and the surrounding paragraph was right.** The entry recording the `exec`-vs-`prepare` defect opened "swapping `exec` for `prepare().run()` turned a loud failure silent" — the exact inverse: `exec` removed drizzle's loud rejection, and the swap restored it. The body, the code and the commit message all had it the right way round, so nothing downstream was misled — but the log is the artifact the HC triages from, and it is the one place the reversed sentence would have been read alone. **Re-read a findings line as someone who has only that line; the summary clause is where a direction inverts, because the body carries the evidence that would have caught it**
